(c) Two disjoint windows. The half-hours are disjoint, so the counts are independent and the probabilities multiply: $$\mathbf{P}=\underbrace{0.251021}_{\text{2 in first}}\times\underbrace{e^{-1.5}}_{=\,0.223130\ \text{(0 in second)}}=0.056010 .$$

(d) Conditioning changes nothing. By the same independence, $\mathbf{P}(N_{\text{2nd}}=0\mid N_{\text{1st}}=2)=\mathbf{P}(N_{\text{2nd}}=0)=e^{-1.5}=0.223130$. Consistency check: $0.056010/0.251021=0.223130$. ✓

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.251021$, (b) $0.997521$, (c) $0.056010$, (d) $0.223130$.

Trap. Using $\lambda\tau=3$ in (a) because the rate is $3$. The Poisson parameter is rate $\times$ length; over half an hour it is $1.5$, and using $3$ would give $e^{-3}3^2/2=0.224042$ — close enough to look plausible, which is what makes it dangerous.

Tool: G4 §3.1 (defining properties, independent disjoint intervals), §3.2 (Poisson PMF of counts) · B&T §6.2

Q85. ★★ · Exponential interarrivals, memorylessness · G4 §3.3

Emergency calls reach a dispatcher as a Poisson process with rate $\lambda=5$ per hour. Let $T$ be the time until the next call, measured in minutes from now.

(a) State the distribution of $T$ and its mean.   (b) Find $\mathbf{P}(T>20)$.   (c) Eight minutes pass with no call. Find the probability of a further wait exceeding $20$ minutes, and the expected further wait.   (d) Find $\mathbf{P}(5<T<15)$.

Solution

Setup. Interarrival times of a Poisson process are exponential with the same rate (G4 §3.3). Converting units: $\lambda=5$/hour $=5/60=0.083333$ per minute, so $T\sim\text{Exp}(1/12)$ with $\mathbb{E}[T]=1/\lambda=12$ minutes and $\mathbf{P}(T>t)=e^{-t/12}$.

(a) $T$ is exponential with rate $0.083333$ per minute; $\mathbb{E}[T]=12$ minutes.

(b) Tail. $\mathbf{P}(T>20)=e^{-20/12}=e^{-1.666667}=0.188876$.

(c) Memorylessness. The exponential is memoryless: for $s,t\ge0$, $\mathbf{P}(T>s+t\mid T>s)=\mathbf{P}(T>t)$. Hence $$\mathbf{P}(T>28\mid T>8)=\mathbf{P}(T>20)=e^{-20/12}=0.188876,$$ identical to (b), and the expected further wait is again $1/\lambda=12$ minutes (so the expected total wait, counted from the original start, is $8+12=20$ minutes). The eight elapsed minutes carry no information because a Poisson process restarts afresh at every fixed time.

(d) An interval probability. Use the two tails: $$\mathbf{P}(5<T<15)=\mathbf{P}(T>5)-\mathbf{P}(T>15)=e^{-5/12}-e^{-15/12}=0.659241-0.286505=0.372736 .$$

Answer. $\;$(a) Exponential, mean $12$ min. (b) $0.188876$. (c) $0.188876$ and $12$ more minutes. (d) $0.372736$.

Trap. In (c), "subtracting the elapsed time" to get $\mathbf{P}(T>20-8)=e^{-12/12}=0.367879$. That treats the wait as a fixed budget being drawn down. Memorylessness says the opposite: after any wait, the remaining time has the original distribution, so the answer is $0.188876$, not $0.367879$.

Tool: G4 §3.3 (exponential interarrivals, memorylessness) · B&T §6.2

Q86. ★★ · Erlang, and which tool to use · G4 §3.4

Same dispatcher as Q85: calls are Poisson with $\lambda=5$ per hour. Let $Y_5$ be the time (in hours from now) of the fifth call.

(a) Name the distribution of $Y_5$, write its PDF, and evaluate the PDF at $t=1$ hour.   (b) Find $\mathbb{E}[Y_5]$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y_5)$.   (c) Find $\mathbf{P}(Y_5>1.5)$ — and explain why the count representation is the right tool rather than integrating the PDF.

Solution

(a) Erlang of order 5. $Y_5=X_1+\dots+X_5$ is a sum of five i.i.d. Exp($\lambda$) interarrivals, which is Erlang of order $5$ (G4 §3.4): $$f_{Y_5}(t)=\frac{\lambda^5 t^4 e^{-\lambda t}}{4!},\qquad t\ge0 .$$ At $t=1$ with $\lambda=5$: $\lambda^5=3125$, $e^{-5}=0.006738$, $4!=24$, so $$f_{Y_5}(1)=\frac{3125\times1\times0.006738}{24}=\frac{21.056}{24}=0.877337\ \text{per hour}.$$

(b) Moments by linearity. Each $X_i$ has mean $1/\lambda=0.2$ h and variance $1/\lambda^2=0.04$ h$^2$, and they are independent, so $$\mathbb{E}[Y_5]=\frac{5}{5}=1\ \text{hour},\qquad \operatorname{var}(Y_5)=\frac{5}{25}=0.2\ \text{h}^2,$$ giving $\sigma=\sqrt{0.2}=0.447214$ h $=26.832816$ minutes.

(c) Counts beat integration. The key equivalence (G4 §3.4) is $$\{Y_5>1.5\}=\{\text{fewer than 5 arrivals by time }1.5\}=\{N_{1.5}\le4\},$$ and $N_{1.5}$ is Poisson with parameter $\lambda\tau=5\times1.5=7.5$. So the answer is a finite sum instead of an integral of $t^4e^{-5t}$ (which needs four integrations by parts). With $e^{-7.5}=0.000553$: $$\mathbf{P}(N_{1.5}\le4)=e^{-7.5}\Big(1+7.5+\frac{7.5^2}{2}+\frac{7.5^3}{6}+\frac{7.5^4}{24}\Big)$$ $$=0.000553+0.004148+0.015555+0.038889+0.072916=0.132062 .$$ A gamma-survival-function evaluation gives $0.132062$ and a $2\times10^6$-draw simulation gives $0.131954$. So a $1.5$-hour wait for the fifth call, though its mean is only $1$ hour, still happens about $13\%$ of the time.

Answer. $\;$(a) Erlang order $5$; $f_{Y_5}(1)=0.877337$/h. (b) $\mathbb{E}[Y_5]=1$ h, $\operatorname{var}(Y_5)=0.2$ h$^2$. (c) $\mathbf{P}(Y_5>1.5)=0.132062$.

Trap. Writing $\{Y_5>1.5\}=\{N_{1.5}<5\}$ but then computing $\mathbf{P}(N_{1.5}\le5)=0.241436$ by including $k=5$. Five arrivals by time $1.5$ means the fifth has already happened, so $k=5$ belongs to the complementary event.

Tool: G4 §3.4 (Erlang PDF and the count equivalence), §3.3 · B&T §6.2

Q87. ★★ · What the small-interval definition actually says · G4 §3.1–3.2

The Poisson process of rate $\lambda$ is often defined by: over an interval of length $\delta$, $\mathbf{P}(0\text{ arrivals})\approx1-\lambda\delta$, $\mathbf{P}(1\text{ arrival})\approx\lambda\delta$, and $\mathbf{P}(2\text{ or more})$ is negligible — all as $\delta\to0$.

(a) With $\lambda=2$ per minute and $\delta=0.001$ minute, compute the exact $\mathbf{P}(\ge1\text{ arrival})$ and compare it to $\lambda\delta$.   (b) Compute the exact $\mathbf{P}(\ge2)$ and the ratio $\mathbf{P}(\ge2)/\delta$; then recompute the ratio at $\delta/2$. What does the comparison establish?   (c) Splitting one minute into $1000$ slots of length $\delta$ and calling each slot a Bernoulli trial with $p=\lambda\delta=0.002$, compare $\mathbf{P}(3\text{ arrivals in the minute})$ under the binomial and the Poisson.

Solution

(a) The linear term. Exactly, $\mathbf{P}(\ge1)=1-e^{-\lambda\delta}$ with $\lambda\delta=2\times0.001=0.002$: $$1-e^{-0.002}=1-0.998002=0.001998 \quad\text{versus}\quad \lambda\delta=0.002 .$$ The relative error is $100\times|0.001998-0.002|/0.001998=0.100033\%$ — the approximation is the first term of $1-e^{-x}=x-x^2/2+\dots$, and the discarded $x^2/2$ is what you see.

(b) "Negligible" means $o(\delta)$, not "small". Exactly, $$\mathbf{P}(\ge2)=1-e^{-\lambda\delta}(1+\lambda\delta)=1-0.998002\times1.002=1.997335\times10^{-6}.$$ The ratio to $\delta$ is $1.997335\times10^{-6}/0.001=0.001997$. Halving to $\delta=0.0005$ gives ratio $0.000999$ — also halved. So $\mathbf{P}(\ge2)/\delta\to0$: multiple arrivals vanish faster than linearly in $\delta$, which is exactly the technical meaning of "negligible" in the definition. The single-arrival probability, by contrast, has ratio $\mathbf{P}(1)/\delta\to\lambda=2$, a nonzero constant. That difference in order is the whole content of the small-interval definition.

(c) Binomial to Poisson. Binomial($1000,0.002$) at $k=3$ gives $0.180628$; Poisson($\lambda\tau=2$) at $k=3$ gives $e^{-2}2^3/3!=0.180447$. They differ by $0.000181$, about one part in a thousand — because $n=1000$ is large and $p=0.002$ is small with $np=2$ fixed, which is precisely the limit that turns the slotted Bernoulli process into the Poisson process (G4 §3.2).

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.001998$ versus $\lambda\delta=0.002$, a $0.10\%$ relative error. (b) $\mathbf{P}(\ge2)=1.9973\times10^{-6}$; the ratio to $\delta$ halves when $\delta$ halves, so $\mathbf{P}(\ge2)=o(\delta)$. (c) $0.180628$ versus $0.180447$.

Trap. Reading "negligible" as "zero" and concluding that a Poisson process can never have two arrivals in a short interval. It can — probability $2\times10^{-6}$ here, not $0$. What is asserted is only that this probability shrinks faster than $\delta$, so it contributes nothing in the limit that builds the process.

Tool: G4 §3.1 (the three defining properties), §3.2 (binomial $\to$ Poisson limit) · B&T §6.2

Q88. ★★ · Competing exponentials · G4 §4.2

A turbine can fail in three independent ways. Time to a bearing failure is exponential with rate $\lambda_1=0.5$/year, to a blade failure exponential with $\lambda_2=0.3$/year, to a controller failure exponential with $\lambda_3=0.2$/year, all independent. The turbine stops at the first of these, at time $T=\min(T_1,T_2,T_3)$.

(a) Find the distribution of $T$ and $\mathbb{E}[T]$.   (b) Find the probability that the blade is the cause.   (c) Find $\mathbf{P}(T>2\text{ years and the controller is the cause})$.   (d) Find $\mathbb{E}[T\mid \text{controller is the cause}]$.

Solution

Setup. Each failure mode is the first arrival of an independent Poisson process; merging them (G4 §4.2) gives a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=\lambda_1+\lambda_2+\lambda_3$, and each merged arrival independently carries a label $i$ with probability $\lambda_i/\lambda$.

(a) Minimum of exponentials. $\mathbf{P}(T>t)=\prod_i\mathbf{P}(T_i>t)=e^{-\lambda_1t}e^{-\lambda_2t}e^{-\lambda_3t}=e^{-\lambda t}$, so $T$ is exponential with $$\lambda=0.5+0.3+0.2=1.0\ \text{per year},\qquad \mathbb{E}[T]=\frac{1}{1.0}=1\ \text{year}.$$ Simulation over $2\times10^6$ turbines gives $\mathbb{E}[T]\approx1.000461$.

(b) Label probability. $\mathbf{P}(\text{blade first})=\lambda_2/\lambda=0.3/1.0=0.3$. Likewise controller $=0.2/1.0=0.2$ (simulated $0.199761$).

(c) The surprise: time and cause are independent. Because the label of a merged arrival is drawn independently of when it occurs, $$\mathbf{P}(T>2\ \text{and controller})=\mathbf{P}(T>2)\,\mathbf{P}(\text{controller})=e^{-1.0\times2}\times0.2=0.135335\times0.2=0.027067 .$$ Simulation gives $0.026880$.

(d) Same independence, read backwards. $\mathbb{E}[T\mid\text{controller first}]=\mathbb{E}[T]=1$ year (simulated $0.998328$). Learning which mode won tells you nothing about when.

Answer. $\;$(a) Exponential rate $1.0$/yr, $\mathbb{E}[T]=1$ year. (b) $0.3$. (c) $0.027067$. (d) $1$ year.

Trap. Answering (d) with "the controller is the slowest mode, mean $1/0.2=5$ years, so conditioning on it should push $\mathbb{E}[T]$ up." It does not: $5$ years is $\mathbb{E}[T_3]$, the controller's own failure time in isolation. Given that the controller won the race, it won at a typical race-winning time, which is $1$ year regardless of the winner's identity.

Tool: G4 §4.2 (merging Poisson processes; independence of arrival time and label) · G3 §1 (minimum of exponentials) · B&T §6.2

Q89. ★★★ · Split streams really are independent · G4 §4.3

Messages arrive at a mail server as a Poisson process of rate $\lambda=12$ per hour. Independently of everything else, each message is spam with probability $p=0.25$ and legitimate otherwise. Over a fixed $2$-hour window let $N_S$ and $N_L$ be the counts of spam and legitimate messages, and $N=N_S+N_L$.

(a) Derive the joint PMF of $(N_S,N_L)$ and show that they are independent Poisson variables.   (b) Compute $\mathbf{P}(N_S=5,\ N_L=20)$.   (c) A student objects: "$N_S+N_L=N$, so a large $N_S$ forces a small $N_L$ — they must be negatively correlated." Resolve the objection.

Solution

Setup. Over $\tau=2$ hours the total count is Poisson with parameter $\lambda\tau=12\times2=24$. Given $N=n$, the $n$ messages are labelled independently, so $N_S\mid N=n$ is Binomial($n,0.25$).

(a) Derivation. Fix integers $s,\ell\ge0$ and put $n=s+\ell$. Condition on the total: $$\mathbf{P}(N_S=s,N_L=\ell)=\mathbf{P}(N=n)\,\mathbf{P}(N_S=s\mid N=n)=e^{-\lambda\tau}\frac{(\lambda\tau)^{n}}{n!}\cdot\binom{n}{s}p^s(1-p)^{\ell}.$$ Expand $\binom{n}{s}=\frac{n!}{s!\,\ell!}$; the $n!$ cancels, and splitting $(\lambda\tau)^n=(\lambda\tau)^s(\lambda\tau)^\ell$ and $e^{-\lambda\tau}=e^{-\lambda\tau p}e^{-\lambda\tau(1-p)}$ gives $$\mathbf{P}(N_S=s,N_L=\ell)=\underbrace{e^{-\lambda\tau p}\frac{(\lambda\tau p)^{s}}{s!}}_{\text{Poisson}(\lambda\tau p)}\times\underbrace{e^{-\lambda\tau(1-p)}\frac{\big(\lambda\tau(1-p)\big)^{\ell}}{\ell!}}_{\text{Poisson}(\lambda\tau(1-p))} .$$ The joint PMF factors into a function of $s$ times a function of $\ell$ for all $(s,\ell)$, which is the definition of independence; and each factor is a Poisson PMF, with parameters $$\lambda\tau p=24\times0.25=6\ \text{(spam)},\qquad \lambda\tau(1-p)=24\times0.75=18\ \text{(legitimate)}.$$

(b) Numbers. By the factorization, $$\mathbf{P}(N_S=5)=e^{-6}\frac{6^5}{5!}=0.160623,\qquad \mathbf{P}(N_L=20)=e^{-18}\frac{18^{20}}{20!}=0.079804,$$ $$\mathbf{P}(N_S=5,N_L=20)=0.160623\times0.079804=0.012818 .$$ Cross-check by the conditional route: $\mathbf{P}(N=25)\mathbf{P}(\text{Bin}(25,0.25)=5)=0.077905\times0.164538=0.012818$. ✓ A $2\times10^6$-window simulation gives $0.012845$, and the sample covariance of $(N_S,N_L)$ is $-0.002$, i.e. zero to simulation noise.

(c) Resolving the objection. The objection would be right if $N$ were fixed: given $N=25$, indeed $N_L=25-N_S$ and the correlation is $-1$. But $N$ is itself random and Poisson. A larger $N_S$ is evidence of a busier window, which raises the expected $N_L$ too; this positive effect through $N$ exactly cancels the negative "shares of a fixed pie" effect, leaving $\operatorname{cov}(N_S,N_L)=0$. The cancellation is exact only for the Poisson total — that is the special property of Poisson splitting. (Check with total variance: $\operatorname{var}(N)=24=\operatorname{var}(N_S)+\operatorname{var}(N_L)+2\operatorname{cov}=6+18+0$. ✓)

Answer. $\;$(a) $N_S\sim$ Poisson($6$) and $N_L\sim$ Poisson($18$), independent. (b) $0.012818$. (c) Conditionally on $N$ they are perfectly negatively dependent, but the randomness of the Poisson total exactly offsets it, giving $\operatorname{cov}(N_S,N_L)=0$ and full independence.

Tool: G4 §4.3 (splitting a Poisson process) · G2 §4 (independence via factorization of a joint PMF) · B&T §6.2

Q90. ★★ · Splitting, then merging · G4 §4.2–4.3

Emails reach inbox 1 as a Poisson process of rate $10$ per hour; each is independently urgent with probability $0.20$. Inbox 2 receives an independent Poisson stream at $4$ per hour.

(a) Find the probability of exactly $4$ urgent emails in a $3$-hour window.   (b) Given that exactly $8$ emails arrived in inbox 1 during one particular hour, find the probability that exactly $2$ of them were urgent.   (c) Considering both inboxes together, find the mean time between consecutive emails and the probability that the next email is from inbox 1.

Solution

(a) Splitting. The urgent substream is itself Poisson with rate $10\times0.20=2$ per hour (G4 §4.3). Over $\tau=3$ hours its parameter is $2\times3=6$, so $$\mathbf{P}(4\text{ urgent})=e^{-6}\frac{6^4}{4!}=0.002479\times\frac{1296}{24}=0.002479\times54=0.133853 .$$

(b) Conditioning on the total makes it binomial. Given that $8$ emails arrived, each is independently urgent with probability $0.20$, so the urgent count is Binomial($8,0.20$): $$\mathbf{P}(2)=\binom{8}{2}(0.20)^2(0.80)^6=28\times0.04\times0.262144=0.293601 .$$

(c) Merging. Two independent Poisson streams merge into a Poisson process of rate $10+4=14$ per hour (G4 §4.2). The interarrival time is exponential with mean $$\frac{1}{14}\ \text{hour}=\frac{60}{14}=4.285714\ \text{minutes},$$ and each merged arrival comes from inbox 1 with probability $\lambda_1/(\lambda_1+\lambda_2)=10/14=0.714286$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.133853$, (b) $0.293601$, (c) $4.285714$ minutes and $0.714286$.

Trap. Answering (b) with the Poisson $\mathbf{P}(N_{\text{urg}}=2)=e^{-2}2^2/2!=0.270671$ for a one-hour window. That is the unconditional answer. Once you are told the exact total, the split count stops being Poisson and becomes binomial with that total as its $n$ — the unconditional Poisson answer $0.270671$ is not the same as $0.293601$.

Tool: G4 §4.3 (splitting), §4.2 (merging and label probabilities) · B&T §6.2

Q91. ★★★ · Random incidence and length bias · G4 §4.5

Trains on a line run with i.i.d. gaps between consecutive departures: a gap is $4$ minutes with probability $0.8$ and $24$ minutes with probability $0.2$. A passenger arrives at the platform at a time unrelated to the schedule (uniform over a long horizon). Let $L$ be the length of the gap the passenger lands in and $W$ the passenger's wait for the next train.

(a) Find the mean gap between trains.   (b) Find the PMF of $L$ and $\mathbb{E}[L]$.   (c) Find $\mathbb{E}[W]$.   (d) Only $20\%$ of gaps are long. What fraction of passengers experience a long gap?

Solution

(a) The gap distribution as scheduled. $\mathbb{E}[X]=0.8(4)+0.2(24)=3.2+4.8=8$ minutes. Over a long horizon of $n$ gaps, total elapsed time is about $8n$ minutes.

(b) Length bias. A uniformly arriving passenger lands in a given gap with probability proportional to that gap's length, not to how often it occurs (G4 §4.5). Out of $n$ gaps, the $0.8n$ short ones occupy $0.8n\times4=3.2n$ minutes and the $0.2n$ long ones occupy $0.2n\times24=4.8n$ minutes. So $$\mathbf{P}(L=4)=\frac{3.2n}{8n}=0.4,\qquad \mathbf{P}(L=24)=\frac{4.8n}{8n}=0.6 .$$ Hence $$\mathbb{E}[L]=0.4(4)+0.6(24)=1.6+14.4=16\ \text{minutes}.$$ The general formula agrees: $\mathbb{E}[L]=\mathbb{E}[X^2]/\mathbb{E}[X]$ with $\mathbb{E}[X^2]=0.8(16)+0.2(576)=12.8+115.2=128$, so $\mathbb{E}[L]=128/8=16$. A simulation over $400\,000$ gaps with $2\times10^6$ uniformly placed passengers gives $\mathbb{E}[L]\approx16.033$.

(c) The wait. Given the gap the passenger lands in has length $\ell$, the arrival instant is uniform inside it, so $\mathbb{E}[W\mid L=\ell]=\ell/2$. By iterated expectations, $$\mathbb{E}[W]=\tfrac12\mathbb{E}[L]=\tfrac12(16)=8\ \text{minutes}.$$ Simulation: $8.018$ minutes.

(d) Passengers versus gaps. $\mathbf{P}(L=24)=0.6$: three passengers in five suffer a long gap, even though only one gap in five is long. The service looks three times worse from the platform than from the timetable, and both descriptions are correct.

Answer. $\;$(a) $8$ min. (b) $L=4$ w.p. $0.4$, $L=24$ w.p. $0.6$; $\mathbb{E}[L]=16$ min. (c) $\mathbb{E}[W]=8$ min. (d) $60\%$.

Trap. Answering (c) with $\mathbb{E}[X]/2=8/2=4$ minutes — "half the average gap". That halves the wrong quantity: you must halve the length-biased gap $\mathbb{E}[L]=16$, not the ordinary gap $\mathbb{E}[X]=8$. The true wait is twice the naive one here.

Tool: G4 §4.5 (random incidence in renewal processes, $\mathbb{E}[L]=\mathbb{E}[X^2]/\mathbb{E}[X]$) · G4 §1.2 · B&T §6.2

Q92. ★★ · Interpretation: the Poisson inspection paradox · G4 §4.4

Buses arrive at a stop as a Poisson process with rate $6$ per hour. You walk up at a time unrelated to the buses. Let $L$ be the length of the interarrival interval that contains your arrival instant.

(a) State the mean interarrival time, your expected wait for the next bus, and the expected time since the previous bus.   (b) Give $\mathbb{E}[L]$ and $\operatorname{var}(L)$.   (c) A commuter says: "$\mathbb{E}[L]=20$ minutes but buses come every $10$ minutes on average — the schedule must be lying, or the process is not Poisson." Respond.

Solution

(a) Two memoryless halves. The rate is $6$/hour, so the mean interarrival time is $60/6=10$ minutes. By memorylessness looking forward, your remaining wait is exponential with mean $10$ minutes; by the same property applied to the reversed process, the elapsed time since the previous bus is also exponential with mean $10$ minutes (G4 §4.4). The two are independent.

(b) Their sum. $L=(\text{time since last})+(\text{time to next})$, a sum of two independent Exp($\lambda$) variables, i.e. Erlang of order $2$: $$\mathbb{E}[L]=\frac{2}{\lambda}=10+10=20\ \text{minutes},\qquad \operatorname{var}(L)=\frac{2}{\lambda^2}=2\times10^2=200\ \text{min}^2,$$ so $\sigma_L=\sqrt{200}=14.142136$ minutes. A $2\times10^6$-draw simulation gives $\mathbb{E}[L]\approx20.005$.

(c) No contradiction — a different sampling scheme. Both statements are true because they average over different populations. "Buses come every $10$ minutes" averages over intervals, each counted once. "$\mathbb{E}[L]=20$" averages over arrival instants, and a long interval covers more instants, so it is more likely to be the one you land in — length bias. The ratio here is exactly $\mathbb{E}[L]/\mathbb{E}[X]=20/10=2$, the Poisson signature $\mathbb{E}[X^2]/\mathbb{E}[X]^2$ for an exponential. Nothing about the process is being misreported.

Answer. $\;$(a) mean gap $10$ min, expected forward wait $10$ min, expected backward time $10$ min. (b) $\mathbb{E}[L]=20$ min, $\operatorname{var}(L)=200$ min$^2$. (c) Both figures are correct; they average over intervals versus over time instants, and length bias makes the second twice the first.

Trap. "Since the mean gap is $10$ minutes and I arrive in the middle, my expected wait is $5$ minutes." Two errors cancel into a wrong answer: the gap you land in averages $20$, and by memorylessness the forward wait is a full $10$ minutes, not half of anything.

Tool: G4 §4.4 (random incidence for the Poisson process) · G4 §3.3 · B&T §6.2

Q93. ★★★ · A Poisson rate that is itself random · G4 §1.3, §3.2

A website's hourly hit rate depends on whether a promotion is running: $\Lambda=20$ per hour with probability $0.7$ (ordinary hour) and $\Lambda=90$ per hour with probability $0.3$ (promoted hour). Given $\Lambda=\lambda$, the number of hits $N$ in that hour is Poisson($\lambda$).

(a) Find $\mathbb{E}[N]$ and $\operatorname{var}(N)$.   (b) Compare $\operatorname{var}(N)$ with the variance of a plain Poisson variable of the same mean, and interpret.   (c) Is $N$ Poisson? Support your answer by computing $\mathbf{P}(N=0)$.

Solution

Setup. $N$ is not Poisson with a fixed parameter; it is a mixture. Condition on $\Lambda$, where the Poisson facts $\mathbb{E}[N\mid\Lambda=\lambda]=\lambda$ and $\operatorname{var}(N\mid\Lambda=\lambda)=\lambda$ apply (G4 §3.2). Thus $\mathbb{E}[N\mid\Lambda]=\Lambda$ and $\operatorname{var}(N\mid\Lambda)=\Lambda$, both random variables.

(a1) Mean, by iterated expectations. $$\mathbb{E}[N]=\mathbb{E}\big[\mathbb{E}[N\mid\Lambda]\big]=\mathbb{E}[\Lambda]=0.7(20)+0.3(90)=14+27=41\ \text{hits}.$$

(a2) Variance, by total variance. Within: $\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(N\mid\Lambda)]=\mathbb{E}[\Lambda]=41$ — the Poisson noise itself. Between: with $\mathbb{E}[\Lambda^2]=0.7(400)+0.3(8100)=280+2430=2710$, $$\operatorname{var}\big(\mathbb{E}[N\mid\Lambda]\big)=\operatorname{var}(\Lambda)=2710-41^2=2710-1681=1029 .$$ Adding, $$\operatorname{var}(N)=41+1029=1070,\qquad \sigma_N=\sqrt{1070}=32.710854 .$$ A $2\times10^6$-hour simulation gives $\mathbb{E}[N]\approx41.017$ and $\operatorname{var}(N)\approx1070.66$.

(b) Overdispersion. A plain Poisson($41$) would have $\operatorname{var}=41$ and $\sigma=6.403124$. The mixture's variance is $1070/41=26.097561$ times larger. Almost all of the excess ($1029$ of $1070$, i.e. $96\%$) is the between term: the dominant uncertainty is not "how many hits at a known rate" but "which regime am I in". Fitting a single Poisson to such data always underestimates the spread — the standard tell of an unmodelled mixture.

(c) Not Poisson. Any Poisson variable has variance equal to its mean; here $1070\ne41$, so $N$ cannot be Poisson. Concretely, $$\mathbf{P}(N=0)=0.7e^{-20}+0.3e^{-90}=0.7\times2.0612\times10^{-9}+\text{(about }10^{-40})=1.442808\times10^{-9},$$ whereas a Poisson($41$) would give $e^{-41}=1.562882\times10^{-18}$ — nine orders of magnitude smaller. The mixture is far more likely to be quiet, because it can be in the low-rate regime.

Answer. $\;\mathbb{E}[N]=41$, $\;\operatorname{var}(N)=41+1029=1070$ ($\sigma_N=32.710854$); $\;26.10\times$ the Poisson variance; $N$ is not Poisson, e.g. $\mathbf{P}(N=0)=1.442808\times10^{-9}$.

Trap. Computing the average rate $41$ and declaring $N\sim$ Poisson($41$) with $\operatorname{var}=41$. Substituting a mean for a random parameter is legitimate for the mean here (because $\mathbb{E}[N\mid\Lambda]$ is linear in $\Lambda$) but destroys the entire between term $1029$ in the variance.

Tool: G4 §1.3 (law of total variance), §3.2 (Poisson mean and variance) · B&T §4.3, §6.2

Q94. ★ · Which tool applies, and why · G4 §5.1

For each of the five situations below, name the single tool from G4 that resolves it and give a one-line reason. Do not compute anything.

(i) A courier job is handled by one of three depots with known probabilities, and each depot has a known mean delivery time; you want the overall mean.   (ii) Daily revenue is the sum of the amounts spent by a random number of customers; you want its variance.   (iii) You show up at a bus stop at a time unrelated to the timetable and want the expected length of the interval you land in.   (iv) Test scores pooled over several classes vary far more than scores within any one class; you want to quantify the split.   (v) A Poisson-arrival hotline has been silent for $10$ minutes; you want the distribution of the additional wait.

Solution

(i) Law of iterated expectations (G4 §1.2). You are given a PMF over "which depot" plus a conditional mean for each; $\mathbb{E}[T]=\sum_s\mathbf{P}(S=s)\mathbb{E}[T\mid S=s]$ is precisely that data assembled. Nothing about variances is needed.

(ii) Random-sum variance (G4 §2.2): $\operatorname{var}(Y)=\mathbb{E}[N]\operatorname{var}(X)+(\mathbb{E}[X])^2\operatorname{var}(N)$. Two independent sources of randomness — the number of terms and each term — and the formula keeps both. Using $\operatorname{var}(Y)=\mathbb{E}[N]\operatorname{var}(X)$ would treat the customer count as fixed.

(iii) Random incidence / length bias (G4 §4.4–4.5). Sampling by time rather than by interval makes long intervals overrepresented in proportion to their length, giving $\mathbb{E}[L]=\mathbb{E}[X^2]/\mathbb{E}[X]\ge\mathbb{E}[X]$.

(iv) Law of total variance (G4 §1.3): $\operatorname{var}(X)=\mathbb{E}[\operatorname{var}(X\mid C)]+\operatorname{var}(\mathbb{E}[X\mid C])$, with $C$ the class. The first term is the within-class scatter, the second the spread of the class averages — exactly the split being asked for.

(v) Memorylessness / fresh start (G4 §3.3, §2.4). The additional wait of a Poisson process, given any elapsed silence, is exponential with the original rate; the silent $10$ minutes carry no information.

Answer. $\;$(i) iterated expectations; (ii) random-sum variance formula; (iii) random incidence / length bias; (iv) law of total variance; (v) memorylessness of the exponential (fresh start).

Tool: G4 §5.1 (the conditioning toolkit on one page) · B&T §4.3–4.4, §6.1–6.2

§5   Markov chains

Drills G5 §1–§5 · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §7.1–§7.4 · every number recomputed in computes/qb_s5.py

Twenty questions on discrete-time Markov chains. The first five ask you to choose the state — to check that a proposed matrix is legitimate, to turn a story into transition probabilities, and (Q99) to catch a process whose obvious state is too small for the Markov property to hold. Q100–Q103 drill classification: recurrent versus transient classes, periodicity, and the two distinct ways the steady-state convergence theorem fails. Q104–Q110 are the steady-state block — balance equations small enough to solve by hand, the frequency interpretation of $\pi_j$ (what it counts, and what it does not count), local balance on birth–death chains, and a finite-buffer queue read through its load factor $\rho$. Q111 is a pure "which system of equations" question: one chain, four questions, four different linear systems. Q112–Q114 are first-step analysis — absorption probabilities, expected time to absorption, and a gambler's-ruin item where the fair-game answer is the trap.

Q95. ★ · Transition matrices · G5 §1.1

A student proposes the following as the transition matrix of a three-state chain on $\{1,2,3\}$, where $p_{ij}=\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=i)$: $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.3&0.3\\0.2&0.5&0.3\\0.4&-0.1&0.7\end{pmatrix}.$$ (a) List every way this fails the definition of a transition matrix. (b) The student also worries that "the columns don't add to 1". Is that a defect? (c) Repair the matrix, touching only the rows that are defective, and confirm your repair.

Solution

Setup. A matrix $P$ is a transition matrix iff (i) $p_{ij}\ge0$ for all $i,j$, and (ii) every row sums to $1$, because row $i$ is the conditional PMF of $X_{n+1}$ given $X_n=i$, and a PMF is nonnegative and sums to $1$.

Step 1 (row sums). Row 1: $0.5+0.3+0.3=1.1$, which exceeds $1$ by $0.1$. Row 2: $0.2+0.5+0.3=1.0\;\checkmark$. Row 3: $0.4+(-0.1)+0.7=1.0$ — it sums correctly, but only because a negative entry cancels an excess.

Step 2 (nonnegativity). $p_{32}=-0.1<0$. A conditional probability cannot be negative, so this is a second, independent violation. So there are exactly two defects: row 1 does not sum to 1, and $p_{32}$ is negative.

Step 3 (part b — the column question). The column sums are $1.1,\;0.7,\;1.3$. This is not a defect. Column $j$ collects $p_{1j},p_{2j},p_{3j}$, which are probabilities of the same event $\{X_{n+1}=j\}$ under three different conditioning events. There is no rule forcing them to add to anything in particular. (Matrices whose columns happen to also sum to 1 are called doubly stochastic and are special — see Q108.)

Step 4 (part c — the repair). Fix row 1 by removing the excess $0.1$ from one entry, say $p_{13}:0.3\to0.2$. Fix row 3 by replacing $p_{32}=-0.1$ with $p_{32}=0.1$ and absorbing the change into $p_{33}:0.7\to0.5$ so the row still sums to 1. The result is $$P'=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.3&0.2\\0.2&0.5&0.3\\0.4&0.1&0.5\end{pmatrix},$$ with row sums $1.0,\;1.0,\;1.0\;\checkmark$ and all entries in $[0,1]\;\checkmark$. Its column sums are $1.1,\;0.9,\;1.0$ — still not 1, and still fine.

Answer. $\;$(a) row 1 sums to $1.1$, and $p_{32}=-0.1<0$; (b) no — column sums are unconstrained; (c) e.g. $p_{13}=0.2$, $p_{32}=0.1$, $p_{33}=0.5$, giving row sums $(1,1,1)$.

Trap. The tempting wrong answer is "row 3 is fine, it sums to 1". Summing to 1 is necessary but not sufficient: nonnegativity is a separate requirement, and a negative entry hidden by a compensating large entry still destroys the probabilistic meaning of the row.

Tool: G5 §1.1 (transition probabilities, row-sum property) · B&T §7.1

Q96. ★ · $n$-step probabilities · G5 §1.3

A chain on $\{1,2,3\}$ has transition matrix $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.4&0.1\\0.2&0.3&0.5\\0&0.6&0.4\end{pmatrix}.$$ Write $r_{ij}(n)=\mathbf{P}(X_n=j\mid X_0=i)$. Compute (a) $\mathbf{P}(X_1=2,\;X_2=3\mid X_0=1)$, (b) $r_{13}(2)$, and (c) $r_{13}(3)$.

Solution

Setup. Part (a) is a single path, so multiply transition probabilities along it. Parts (b),(c) need the Chapman–Kolmogorov recursion $r_{ij}(n)=\sum_{k}r_{ik}(n-1)\,p_{kj}$, which is total probability over the state at time $n-1$ with the Markov property used to drop the earlier history.

Step 1 (part a). By the multiplication rule and the Markov property, $$\mathbf{P}(X_1=2,X_2=3\mid X_0=1)=p_{12}\,p_{23}=(0.4)(0.5)=0.2 .$$

Step 2 (part b). Sum over the intermediate state $k$ at time 1: $$r_{13}(2)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}p_{1k}p_{k3}=(0.5)(0.1)+(0.4)(0.5)+(0.1)(0.4)=0.05+0.2+0.04=0.29 .$$ The middle term $0.2$ is exactly part (a) — one of three disjoint routes.

Step 3 (the full two-step row). For part (c) we need all of row 1 of $P^2$: $$r_{11}(2)=(0.5)(0.5)+(0.4)(0.2)+(0.1)(0)=0.25+0.08+0=0.33,$$ $$r_{12}(2)=(0.5)(0.4)+(0.4)(0.3)+(0.1)(0.6)=0.20+0.12+0.06=0.38,$$ and $r_{13}(2)=0.29$ from Step 2. Check: $0.33+0.38+0.29=1.00\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). Apply the recursion once more, now summing over the state at time 2: $$r_{13}(3)=\sum_{k=1}^{3}r_{1k}(2)\,p_{k3}=(0.33)(0.1)+(0.38)(0.5)+(0.29)(0.4)=0.033+0.19+0.116=0.339 .$$ A Monte-Carlo run of $400{,}000$ paths gives $0.2901$ and $0.3372$ for (b) and (c), confirming both.

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.2$, (b) $r_{13}(2)=0.29$, (c) $r_{13}(3)=0.339$.

Trap. A careless reader reports $0.2$ for part (b) as well, having found "the" path $1\to2\to3$. $r_{13}(2)$ is the probability of being at 3 at time 2 by any route, including $1\to1\to3$ and $1\to3\to3$; those add another $0.05+0.04=0.09$.

Tool: G5 §1.3 (Chapman–Kolmogorov recursion) · B&T §7.1

Q97. ★★ · Building a chain from a story · G5 §1.1–1.3

Every Monday a streaming subscriber is in one of three states: Active, Paused, or Cancelled. From Active, next Monday she is Active with probability $0.80$, Paused with probability $0.15$, and Cancelled with probability $0.05$. From Paused, she is Active with probability $0.30$, Paused with probability $0.55$, and Cancelled with probability $0.15$. Cancellation is permanent. (a) Write the transition matrix with states ordered $(A,P,C)$. (b) Given that she is Active this Monday, what is the probability she is Cancelled three Mondays later? (c) A colleague says "the cancellation rate is $5\%$ per week, so over three weeks it is about $3\times0.05=0.15$." Why is his number wrong, and in which direction?

Solution

Setup. Each sentence of the story is one row of $P$. "Cancellation is permanent" means $C$ is absorbing: $p_{CC}=1$ and $p_{CA}=p_{CP}=0$.

Step 1 (part a). $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.80&0.15&0.05\\0.30&0.55&0.15\\0&0&1\end{pmatrix},$$ row sums $0.80+0.15+0.05=1$, $0.30+0.55+0.15=1$, $0+0+1=1\;\checkmark$.

Step 2 (two steps from A). Using $r_{Aj}(2)=\sum_k p_{Ak}p_{kj}$, $$r_{AA}(2)=(0.80)(0.80)+(0.15)(0.30)+(0.05)(0)=0.64+0.045=0.685,$$ $$r_{AP}(2)=(0.80)(0.15)+(0.15)(0.55)+(0.05)(0)=0.12+0.0825=0.2025,$$ $$r_{AC}(2)=(0.80)(0.05)+(0.15)(0.15)+(0.05)(1)=0.04+0.0225+0.05=0.1125 .$$ Check: $0.685+0.2025+0.1125=1.0000\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part b — three steps). Recurse once more into column $C$: $$r_{AC}(3)=r_{AA}(2)\,p_{AC}+r_{AP}(2)\,p_{PC}+r_{AC}(2)\,p_{CC}$$ $$=(0.685)(0.05)+(0.2025)(0.15)+(0.1125)(1)=0.034250+0.030375+0.112500=0.177125 .$$ Note the third term: probability already absorbed stays absorbed, which is why $p_{CC}=1$ carries it forward. A Monte-Carlo run of $400{,}000$ subscribers gives $0.1765\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). The colleague's $0.15$ is wrong for two compounding reasons, which push in opposite directions. Adding $0.05$ three times double-counts: after week 1 the subscriber may already be Cancelled, so the same event is counted again. That inflates the estimate. But he also ignores the Paused state, which cancels at the higher rate $0.15$ and which the subscriber enters with probability $0.15$ per week. Numerically the net effect is that the true value $0.177125$ exceeds $0.15$: the Paused channel more than offsets the double count.

Answer. $\;$(a) as displayed; (b) $\mathbf{P}(X_3=C\mid X_0=A)=0.177125$; (c) $0.15$ neither accounts for absorption already having occurred nor for the higher hazard from Paused, and the true answer is larger.

Trap. Writing $1-(0.95)^3=0.142625$ is the "improved" wrong answer: it fixes the double count but still assumes the cancellation probability is $0.05$ every week regardless of state, which is false as soon as the subscriber pauses.

Tool: G5 §1.1 (modeling recipe), §1.3 ($n$-step recursion) · B&T §7.1

Q98. ★★ · Initial distributions and running the chain backwards · G5 §1.3

A web server is Up (state 1) or Down (state 2) each hour, with $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.9&0.1\\0.4&0.6\end{pmatrix},$$ and at hour $0$ it is Up with probability $0.3$ and Down with probability $0.7$. (a) Compute $\mathbf{P}(X_2=1)$. (b) Compute $\mathbf{P}(X_0=1\mid X_1=1)$. (c) A classmate says "the Markov property says the past doesn't matter, so $\mathbf{P}(X_0=1\mid X_1=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_0=1)=0.3$." Diagnose the error.

Solution

Setup. Write the time-$n$ distribution as a row vector $\pi^{(n)}=(\mathbf{P}(X_n=1),\mathbf{P}(X_n=2))$. Then $\pi^{(n+1)}=\pi^{(n)}P$, which is the total probability theorem applied to the state one step earlier. Part (b) runs the other way and therefore needs Bayes' rule.

Step 1 (one step). $$\mathbf{P}(X_1=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_0=1)p_{11}+\mathbf{P}(X_0=2)p_{21}=(0.3)(0.9)+(0.7)(0.4)=0.27+0.28=0.55,$$ so $\pi^{(1)}=(0.55,\,0.45)$.

Step 2 (part a — second step). $$\mathbf{P}(X_2=1)=(0.55)(0.9)+(0.45)(0.4)=0.495+0.180=0.675 .$$

Step 3 (part b — Bayes). The joint probability of the numerator event was already computed in Step 1 as the first term: $$\mathbf{P}(X_0=1,\;X_1=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_0=1)\,p_{11}=(0.3)(0.9)=0.27 .$$ Divide by $\mathbf{P}(X_1=1)=0.55$: $$\mathbf{P}(X_0=1\mid X_1=1)=\frac{0.27}{0.55}=0.490909 .$$ (Complement: $0.28/0.55=0.509091$; the two sum to $1\;\checkmark$.) A Monte-Carlo run of $600{,}000$ paths gives $0.4905\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c — the diagnosis). The Markov property says the future is conditionally independent of the past given the present: $\mathbf{P}(X_{n+1}=j\mid X_n=i,\text{ earlier})=p_{ij}$. It says nothing about the past given the future. Here observing $X_1=1$ is evidence about $X_0$, because state 1 is much easier to reach from state 1 ($0.9$) than from state 2 ($0.4$); that evidence raises the posterior on $X_0=1$ from the prior $0.3$ to $0.490909$. Independence of past and future would require $\mathbf{P}(X_0=1,X_1=1)=\mathbf{P}(X_0=1)\mathbf{P}(X_1=1)=(0.3)(0.55)=0.165$, but the true joint is $0.27$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $\mathbf{P}(X_2=1)=0.675$; (b) $\mathbf{P}(X_0=1\mid X_1=1)=0.490909$; (c) the Markov property conditions on the present and constrains the future, not the past.

Trap. $0.3$ is the tempting answer, and it is the prior, not the posterior. A second trap in part (a) is reading $r_{11}(2)=0.85$ off $P^2$ and reporting it — that is $\mathbf{P}(X_2=1\mid X_0=1)$, which ignores the initial distribution.

Tool: G5 §1.3 (initial distribution, $n$-step probabilities) · G1 §2.4 (Bayes' rule) · B&T §7.1

Q99. ★★★ · When the obvious state is not a state · G5 §1.1

Two coins sit on a table: coin $A$ lands heads with probability $0.8$, coin $B$ with probability $0.3$. At time $0$ you pick one of the two uniformly at random, you are not told which, and from then on you flip that same coin forever, the flips being conditionally independent given the coin. Let $X_n\in\{H,T\}$ be the outcome of flip $n$, $n=1,2,\dots$ (a) Show that $\{X_n\}$ is not a Markov chain on $\{H,T\}$ by comparing $\mathbf{P}(X_2=H\mid X_1=H)$ with $\mathbf{P}(X_3=H\mid X_2=H,X_1=H)$. (b) Enlarge the state so that the Markov property holds, write the transition matrix, and classify the resulting chain.

Solution

Setup. Let $C\in\{A,B\}$ be the identity of the chosen coin, $\mathbf{P}(C=A)=0.5$. Given $C$, the flips are i.i.d. Bernoulli. Unconditionally the flips are exchangeable but not independent, because every flip carries information about $C$, and $C$ affects every future flip.

Step 1 (the joint probabilities of head runs). Condition on the coin and use conditional independence: $$\mathbf{P}(X_1=H)=(0.5)(0.8)+(0.5)(0.3)=0.40+0.15=0.55,$$ $$\mathbf{P}(X_1=X_2=H)=(0.5)(0.8)^2+(0.5)(0.3)^2=(0.5)(0.64)+(0.5)(0.09)=0.320+0.045=0.365,$$ $$\mathbf{P}(X_1=X_2=X_3=H)=(0.5)(0.8)^3+(0.5)(0.3)^3=(0.5)(0.512)+(0.5)(0.027)=0.2560+0.0135=0.2695 .$$

Step 2 (part a — the two conditionals). $$\mathbf{P}(X_2=H\mid X_1=H)=\frac{0.365}{0.55}=0.663636,\qquad \mathbf{P}(X_3=H\mid X_2=H,X_1=H)=\frac{0.2695}{0.365}=0.738356 .$$ The same "current state" $H$ gives two different predictions, differing by $0.074720$. If $\{X_n\}$ were Markov on $\{H,T\}$ both would equal $p_{HH}$. So the Markov property fails.

Step 3 (why, in words). The extra head in the conditioning event is evidence about the coin. By Bayes' rule, $$\mathbf{P}(C=A\mid X_1=X_2=H)=\frac{(0.5)(0.64)}{0.365}=\frac{0.320}{0.365}=0.876712,$$ and then the predictive probability is the mixture $$(0.876712)(0.8)+(1-0.876712)(0.3)=0.701370+0.036986=0.738356\;\checkmark,$$ matching Step 2. The single symbol $H$ is a lossy summary: it does not record how much evidence has accumulated about $C$.

Step 4 (part b — the repair). Take the state to be the pair $Y_n=(C,X_n)$, four states $(A,H),(A,T),(B,H),(B,T)$. Since $C$ never changes and the next flip depends only on $C$, $$p_{(A,\cdot)\to(A,H)}=0.8,\quad p_{(A,\cdot)\to(A,T)}=0.2,\quad p_{(B,\cdot)\to(B,H)}=0.3,\quad p_{(B,\cdot)\to(B,T)}=0.7,$$ all other entries $0$. Ordering the states $(A,H),(A,T),(B,H),(B,T)$, $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.8&0.2&0&0\\0.8&0.2&0&0\\0&0&0.3&0.7\\0&0&0.3&0.7\end{pmatrix},$$ row sums all $1\;\checkmark$. This is Markov because the transition law depends on the past only through $(C,X_n)$, and in fact only through $C$.

Step 5 (classification). $P$ is block diagonal: $\{(A,H),(A,T)\}$ and $\{(B,H),(B,T)\}$ are two separate recurrent classes, each aperiodic, with no transient states. Consequently the limit remembers the start: $P^{10}$ from $(A,H)$ is $(0.8,\,0.2,\,0,\,0)$, whereas from $(B,H)$ it is $(0,\,0,\,0.3,\,0.7)$. That is exactly the modeling content of the story — you never learn which coin you have with certainty, and the chain never forgets it.

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.663636\ne0.738356$, so $\{X_n\}$ is not Markov on $\{H,T\}$; (b) $Y_n=(C,X_n)$ is Markov, with the block-diagonal $P$ above — two aperiodic recurrent classes, no transient states.

Trap. "The flips are independent tosses of one coin, so of course the process is Markov (indeed i.i.d.)." They are independent only conditionally on $C$. Marginalizing over an unknown, permanent parameter creates dependence that reaches arbitrarily far back — the whole history matters, which is why the repair must put the parameter into the state.

Tool: G5 §1.1 (choosing a state; Markov property test) · G1 §2.4 (Bayes' rule) · B&T §7.1

Q100. ★ · Recurrent and transient classes · G5 §2.1–2.3

A chain on $\{1,\dots,6\}$ has the nonzero transitions $$p_{11}=0.2,\;p_{12}=0.3,\;p_{13}=0.5;\qquad p_{21}=0.4,\;p_{25}=0.6;$$ $$p_{34}=1;\qquad p_{43}=0.7,\;p_{44}=0.3;\qquad p_{56}=1;\qquad p_{65}=1 .$$ Identify the recurrent classes, the transient states, and the period of each recurrent class.

Solution

Setup. State $j$ is accessible from $i$ if $r_{ij}(n)>0$ for some $n\ge0$. State $i$ is recurrent if every $j$ accessible from $i$ can reach $i$ back; otherwise it is transient. Recurrent states split into classes of mutually accessible states.

Step 1 (accessible sets). From 3 you go to 4 and from 4 back to 3, and nothing else is reachable: the accessible set of 3 is $\{3,4\}$. Likewise 5 goes to 6 and 6 to 5, so the accessible set of 5 is $\{5,6\}$. Both sets are closed (no arrow leaves them), so $\{3,4\}$ and $\{5,6\}$ are recurrent classes.

Step 2 (the transient states). From 1 you can reach 3 (probability $0.5$), and from 3 there is no path back to 1, since $\{3,4\}$ is closed. Hence 1 is transient. From 2 you can reach 5 with probability $0.6$, and $\{5,6\}$ is closed, so 2 is transient too. States 1 and 2 communicate with each other ($1\to2$ and $2\to1$), so they form one transient class $\{1,2\}$.

Step 3 (periods). Class $\{3,4\}$: state 4 has a self-transition $p_{44}=0.3>0$, so a return to 4 is possible in $1$ step and also in $2$ steps ($4\to3\to4$); $\gcd(1,2)=1$, so the class is aperiodic. Class $\{5,6\}$: every arrow goes $5\to6$ or $6\to5$, so returns to 5 occur only at even times; $\gcd$ of the possible return times is $2$, so this class has period $2$.

Step 4 (what this predicts). The chain has two recurrent classes, so the limit of $r_{ij}(n)$ depends on the starting state, and one of the classes is periodic, so for starts leading into $\{5,6\}$ the limit fails to exist at all. Numerically, from state 1, $r_{1\cdot}(200)=(0,\,0,\,0.302768,\,0.432526,\,0.215686,\,0.049020)$ while $r_{1\cdot}(201)=(0,\,0,\,0.302768,\,0.432526,\,0.049020,\,0.215686)$ — the $\{3,4\}$ entries have settled, the $\{5,6\}$ entries oscillate forever.

Answer. $\;$Recurrent classes $\{3,4\}$ (aperiodic) and $\{5,6\}$ (period 2); transient states $1$ and $2$.

Trap. Calling $\{1,2\}$ a recurrent class because 1 and 2 communicate with each other. Communication is not enough — the class must also be closed, and $\{1,2\}$ leaks out through $p_{13}$ and $p_{25}$.

Tool: G5 §2.1–2.2 (accessibility, recurrent classes), §2.3 (periodicity) · B&T §7.2

Q101. ★★ · Periodicity · G5 §2.3

A chain on $\{1,2,3,4\}$ has $p_{12}=1$, $p_{23}=1$, $p_{31}=0.6$, $p_{34}=0.4$, $p_{41}=1$, and no other nonzero entries. (a) Is the chain irreducible (one recurrent class)? (b) Find its period. (c) True or false, with reason: "a chain in which no state has a self-transition must be periodic." (d) For which $n\le12$ is $r_{11}(n)=0$?

Solution

Setup. The period of a recurrent class containing state $i$ is $d=\gcd\{n\ge1:r_{ii}(n)>0\}$; every state in a class has the same period. The quickest route to $d$ is to find the lengths of the cycles through one state and take their gcd.

Step 1 (part a). From 1 you reach 2, 3, then 1 again, and also 4 via $3\to4$; from 4 you return to 1. So all four states are mutually accessible and the chain is irreducible — one recurrent class, no transient states.

Step 2 (part b — the cycles through state 1). There are exactly two simple cycles through 1: $$1\to2\to3\to1\quad\text{(length }3),\qquad 1\to2\to3\to4\to1\quad\text{(length }4).$$ Every return to 1 is a concatenation of these, so the set of possible return times is $\{3a+4b:a,b\ge0,\;a+b\ge1\}=\{3,4,6,7,8,9,10,\dots\}$. Its gcd is $\gcd(3,4)=1$, so the chain is aperiodic, $d=1$.

Step 3 (part c). False, and this chain is the counterexample: no state has a self-transition, yet the chain is aperiodic. Aperiodicity needs only two return times whose gcd is 1; a self-transition is one convenient way to get that (a return time of 1 forces $d=1$) but it is not necessary.

Step 4 (part d). The impossible return times are the positive integers not representable as $3a+4b$: $n=1,2,5$. Direct computation of $r_{11}(n)$ for $n=1,\dots,12$ gives $$0,\;0,\;0.6,\;0.4,\;0,\;0.36,\;0.48,\;0.16,\;0.216,\;0.432,\;0.288,\;0.1936,$$ zero exactly at $n=1,2,5$ and strictly positive for every $n\ge6\;\checkmark$ — the hallmark of an aperiodic chain. Consistently, $r_{1\cdot}(100)$ and $r_{1\cdot}(101)$ both equal $(0.294118,\,0.294118,\,0.294118,\,0.117647)=(5/17,5/17,5/17,2/17)$: the limit exists and does not oscillate.

Answer. $\;$(a) yes, irreducible; (b) period $d=\gcd(3,4)=1$, aperiodic; (c) false — this chain has no self-transitions and is aperiodic; (d) $r_{11}(n)=0$ exactly for $n=1,2,5$.

Trap. Reading the "obvious" loop $1\to2\to3\to1$ and answering "period 3". The period is the gcd over all return times, and the branch $3\to4$ supplies a return of length 4, which collapses the gcd to 1.

Tool: G5 §2.3 (periodicity, gcd of return times) · B&T §7.2

Q102. ★★ · The two ways convergence fails · G5 §2.4

The steady-state convergence theorem says: if a chain has a single recurrent class and that class is aperiodic, then $r_{ij}(n)\to\pi_j$ for every $i$. For each of the two chains below, say which hypothesis fails, and describe precisely what $r_{ij}(n)$ actually does.

Chain A on $\{1,2\}$: $p_{12}=p_{21}=1$.    Chain B on $\{1,2,3,4\}$: $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.7&0.3&0&0\\0.4&0.6&0&0\\0&0&0.5&0.5\\0&0&0.1&0.9\end{pmatrix}.$$

Solution

Setup. Two hypotheses, two failure modes. Periodicity destroys the existence of the limit; multiple recurrent classes destroy its independence of $i$.

Step 1 (Chain A: the aperiodicity hypothesis fails). The chain is irreducible — a single recurrent class $\{1,2\}$ — so that hypothesis holds. But every return to 1 takes an even number of steps, so $d=2$. Concretely $$r_{11}(n)=\begin{cases}1,&n\text{ even},\\0,&n\text{ odd},\end{cases}$$ so $r_{11}(10)=1$ and $r_{11}(11)=0$: no limit exists. What is still true is that the balance equations $\pi_1=\pi_2$, $\pi_1+\pi_2=1$ have the unique solution $\pi=(0.5,0.5)$, and the frequency interpretation survives: the long-run fraction of time in state 1 is $0.5$. Averaging the first $2000$ powers of $P$ gives row 1 equal to $(0.5,\,0.5)$ to six decimals $\checkmark$. So $\pi$ is still meaningful as a time-average, just not as a limit of $r_{ij}(n)$.

Step 2 (Chain B: the single-class hypothesis fails). $P$ is block diagonal, so $\{1,2\}$ and $\{3,4\}$ are two separate closed classes, each recurrent and each aperiodic (self-transitions $p_{11},p_{22},p_{33},p_{44}>0$). Every $r_{ij}(n)$ converges — but to a limit that depends on where you started.

Step 3 (the two limits). Solve balance inside each block. Block $\{1,2\}$: $0.3\pi_1=0.4\pi_2$, so $\pi_1/\pi_2=4/3$ and with $\pi_1+\pi_2=1$, $$(\pi_1,\pi_2)=(4/7,\,3/7)=(0.571429,\,0.428571).$$ Block $\{3,4\}$: $0.5\pi_3=0.1\pi_4$, so $\pi_3/\pi_4=1/5$ and $$(\pi_3,\pi_4)=(1/6,\,5/6)=(0.166667,\,0.833333).$$ Numerically $P^{200}$ has rows 1 and 2 equal to $(0.571429,\,0.428571,\,0,\,0)$ and rows 3 and 4 equal to $(0,\,0,\,0.166667,\,0.833333)\;\checkmark$. In particular $r_{13}(n)\to0$ while $r_{33}(n)\to0.166667$: the "steady state" is not one vector but two, selected by the initial state.

Answer. $\;$Chain A fails aperiodicity ($d=2$): $r_{11}(n)$ oscillates $1,0,1,0,\dots$ and has no limit, though the time-average frequencies are still $(0.5,0.5)$. Chain B fails the single-recurrent-class hypothesis: every $r_{ij}(n)$ converges, but to $(4/7,3/7,0,0)$ from states 1,2 and to $(0,0,1/6,5/6)$ from states 3,4.

Trap. Solving $\pi=\pi P$ for Chain B and reporting "the" answer. That system is degenerate here — any mixture $\alpha(4/7,3/7,0,0)+(1-\alpha)(0,0,1/6,5/6)$ satisfies it, so the balance equations plus normalization no longer pin down a unique $\pi$. Uniqueness of the solution is a consequence of having one recurrent class, not an assumption you may take for granted.

Tool: G5 §2.4 (steady-state convergence theorem and its two failure modes) · B&T §7.3

Q103. ★★★ · True or false, with reason · G5 §2.1–2.4

Decide true or false and justify each. (a) Every finite-state Markov chain has at least one recurrent state. (b) If a chain has exactly one recurrent class, then $r_{ij}(n)$ converges to a limit that does not depend on $i$. (c) If a chain has a single aperiodic recurrent class together with some transient states, then $\pi_j=0$ for every transient $j$.

Solution

Setup. Recall: $i$ is recurrent iff every state accessible from $i$ can reach $i$ back; $\pi_j$ is the unique solution of the balance equations $\pi_j=\sum_k\pi_kp_{kj}$ with $\sum_j\pi_j=1$, and equals the long-run fraction of time spent in $j$.

Step 1 (part a — TRUE). Suppose every state were transient. Start at any state $i_1$. Because $i_1$ is transient, there is a state $i_2$ accessible from $i_1$ that cannot reach $i_1$ back. Then the accessible set of $i_2$ is a strict subset of that of $i_1$: it is contained in it (anything reachable from $i_2$ is reachable from $i_1$) and it omits $i_1$. Repeat from $i_2$. Each step strictly shrinks a subset of a finite state space, so the process must terminate — and it terminates exactly at a state that is not transient. Hence at least one recurrent state exists. (This argument uses finiteness essentially; an infinite chain such as the unbiased random walk on $\mathbb{Z}$ modified to drift right can have all states transient.)

Step 2 (part b — FALSE). The single-class hypothesis is not sufficient; aperiodicity is also needed. Counterexample: the two-state chain $p_{12}=p_{21}=1$ is irreducible, hence has exactly one recurrent class $\{1,2\}$, yet $$r_{11}(200)=1,\qquad r_{11}(201)=0,$$ so $r_{11}(n)$ has no limit at all. The correct statement adds "and that class is aperiodic".

Step 3 (part c — TRUE). $\pi_j$ is the long-run fraction of time in $j$. A transient state is visited only finitely many times with probability 1, so the number of visits in $n$ steps stays bounded while $n\to\infty$, and the fraction tends to $0$. Algebraically the balance equations force the same conclusion, since the unique normalized solution puts all mass on the recurrent class. Example: with $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.5&0\\0&0.6&0.4\\0&0.3&0.7\end{pmatrix}$$ state 1 is transient (it can reach the closed class $\{2,3\}$ but not return) and the balance equations give $$\pi=(0,\;3/7,\;4/7)=(0,\;0.428571,\;0.571429),$$ confirmed by $r_{1\cdot}(200)=(0,\,0.428571,\,0.571429)$.

Answer. $\;$(a) True; (b) False — aperiodicity is also required, e.g. $p_{12}=p_{21}=1$; (c) True, $\pi_j=0$ for transient $j$.

Trap. In (c), reading $\pi_j=0$ as "the chain never visits $j$". It does visit $j$ — possibly many times, and $\mathbf{P}(X_0=j)$ may even be 1 — but the number of visits is finite, so its share of an infinitely long run is zero. $\pi_j$ measures frequency, not possibility.

Tool: G5 §2.1 (recurrence), §2.4 (convergence theorem), §3.3 (frequency interpretation) · B&T §7.2–7.3

Q104. ★ · Balance equations, two states · G5 §3.1–3.2

A machine is Working (state 1) or Under repair (state 2) on each day, with $p_{12}=0.15$ and $p_{21}=0.25$ (so $p_{11}=0.85$, $p_{22}=0.75$). (a) Find the steady-state probabilities. (b) Over $400$ days, about how many do you expect the machine to be working? (c) What is the mean recurrence time of state 2?

Solution

Setup. The chain is irreducible (both $p_{12},p_{21}>0$) and aperiodic ($p_{11}>0$), so a unique $\pi$ exists and $r_{ij}(n)\to\pi_j$.

Step 1 (balance). The balance equation for state 1 reads $$\pi_1=\pi_1p_{11}+\pi_2p_{21}=0.85\,\pi_1+0.25\,\pi_2 .$$ Move $0.85\pi_1$ across: $0.15\,\pi_1=0.25\,\pi_2$. (In two states this is also the cut equation: flow $1\to2$ equals flow $2\to1$.)

Step 2 (normalize). From $0.15\pi_1=0.25\pi_2$ we get $\pi_1=\frac{0.25}{0.15}\pi_2=\frac{5}{3}\pi_2=1.666667\,\pi_2$. Substituting into $\pi_1+\pi_2=1$: $$\tfrac53\pi_2+\pi_2=\tfrac83\pi_2=1\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_2=\tfrac38=0.375,\qquad \pi_1=\tfrac58=0.625 .$$ Check against the other balance equation: $\pi_2=0.15(0.625)+0.75(0.375)=0.09375+0.28125=0.375\;\checkmark$. Also $P^{50}$ has first row $(0.625,\,0.375)\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part b). By the frequency interpretation, the long-run fraction of days working is $\pi_1=0.625$, so over $400$ days the expected number is $400(0.625)=250$ days.

Step 4 (part c). The mean recurrence time of a state is the reciprocal of its steady-state probability: $$t_2^{*}=\frac{1}{\pi_2}=\frac{1}{0.375}=\frac83=2.666667\ \text{days}.$$ Reading: repairs recur on average once every $2.67$ days — consistent with the machine being under repair $37.5\%$ of the time.

Answer. $\;\pi=(0.625,\,0.375)$; about $250$ working days out of $400$; $t_2^{*}=8/3=2.666667$ days.

Trap. Answering $\pi_1=0.15/(0.15+0.25)=0.375$ — the ratio is inverted. The state that is hard to leave (small $p_{12}$) gets the large steady-state probability, so $\pi_1=p_{21}/(p_{12}+p_{21})=0.25/0.40=0.625$.

Tool: G5 §3.1–3.2 (balance equations), §4.4 (mean recurrence time $t_i^{*}=1/\pi_i$) · B&T §7.3

Q105. ★★ · Balance equations, three states · G5 §3.2–3.3

A kiosk holds at most 2 sandwiches. Let $X_n$ be the number left at the end of day $n$. If the kiosk ends a day empty, the supplier refills it to 2 the next morning; otherwise no delivery is made. Daily demand $D$ is $0,1,2$ with probabilities $0.3,0.5,0.2$, independent across days, and unmet demand is lost. (a) Write the transition matrix on $\{0,1,2\}$. (b) Solve the balance equations. (c) On what fraction of days is a delivery made, and how many deliveries per year (365 days) is that?

Solution

Setup. The day starts with $s=2$ if $X_n=0$ (refilled) and $s=X_n$ otherwise; then $X_{n+1}=\max(s-D,0)$. Since $D$ is independent of the past, $X_{n+1}$ depends on the history only through $X_n$: the Markov property holds.

Step 1 (part a — the rows). From $X_n=0$: the day starts with $s=2$, so $X_{n+1}=2$ if $D=0$ (prob $0.3$), $=1$ if $D=1$ (prob $0.5$), $=0$ if $D=2$ (prob $0.2$). Row $0=(0.2,\,0.5,\,0.3)$. From $X_n=1$: $s=1$, so $X_{n+1}=1$ if $D=0$ (prob $0.3$) and $X_{n+1}=0$ if $D\ge1$ (prob $0.5+0.2=0.7$). Row $1=(0.7,\,0.3,\,0)$. From $X_n=2$: $s=2$, identical to the first case. Row $2=(0.2,\,0.5,\,0.3)$. So $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.2&0.5&0.3\\0.7&0.3&0\\0.2&0.5&0.3\end{pmatrix},$$ row sums all $1\;\checkmark$. The chain is irreducible and aperiodic ($p_{00}>0$), so $\pi$ is unique.

Step 2 (part b — exploit the repeated row). Rows 0 and 2 are identical, which simplifies everything. Write $s=\pi_0+\pi_2$. The balance equation for state 2 is $$\pi_2=0.3\pi_0+0\cdot\pi_1+0.3\pi_2=0.3(\pi_0+\pi_2)=0.3\,s .$$ The balance equation for state 1 is $$\pi_1=0.5\pi_0+0.3\pi_1+0.5\pi_2=0.5\,s+0.3\pi_1\;\Longrightarrow\;0.7\,\pi_1=0.5\,s\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_1=\tfrac57\,s .$$

Step 3 (normalize). Since $s+\pi_1=1$, $$s+\tfrac57 s=\tfrac{12}{7}s=1\;\Longrightarrow\;s=\tfrac{7}{12},\qquad \pi_1=\tfrac57\cdot\tfrac7{12}=\tfrac{5}{12}=0.416667 .$$ Then $\pi_2=0.3\,s=0.3\cdot\frac7{12}=\frac{21}{120}=\frac{7}{40}=0.175$, and $$\pi_0=s-\pi_2=\tfrac{7}{12}-\tfrac{7}{40}=\tfrac{70}{120}-\tfrac{21}{120}=\tfrac{49}{120}=0.408333 .$$ Check the remaining balance equation for state 0: $0.2(0.408333)+0.7(0.416667)+0.2(0.175)=0.081667+0.291667+0.035=0.408333\;\checkmark$, and $\frac{49+50+21}{120}=1\;\checkmark$. A simulation of $200{,}000$ chains run 400 steps gives $(0.4092,\,0.4176,\,0.1732)\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). A delivery is made on the morning following a day that ended empty, so the long-run fraction of days with a delivery equals the long-run fraction of days ending empty, $\pi_0=0.408333$. Over a year: $365(0.408333)=149.0417$, about $149$ deliveries.

Answer. $\;\pi=(49/120,\,5/12,\,7/40)=(0.408333,\,0.416667,\,0.175)$; deliveries on $40.8333\%$ of days, about $149$ per year.

Trap. Defining the state as "stock at the start of the day, after any delivery". That state can never equal 0 (a 0 is immediately refilled to 2), so the naive three-state chain degenerates and the delivery frequency has to be recovered indirectly. Choosing the end-of-day stock — the quantity that actually determines the next day's dynamics — is what makes the model clean.

Tool: G5 §3.2 (solving balance equations), §3.3 (frequency interpretation) · B&T §7.3

Q106. ★★ · What $\pi_j$ actually counts · G5 §3.3

A chain on $\{1,2,3\}$ has $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.60&0.20&0.20\\0.40&0.50&0.10\\0.40&0.25&0.35\end{pmatrix},$$ and you are told its steady-state vector is $\pi=(0.5,\,0.3,\,0.2)$. (a) Verify $\pi$ by checking the balance equation for state 2. (b) In a long run of $1000$ transitions, about how many time steps are spent in state 1? (c) About how many of those $1000$ transitions are transitions from 2 to 3? (d) What is the mean recurrence time of state 3?

Solution

Setup. Two distinct frequency statements. $\pi_j$ is the long-run fraction of time in $j$; $\pi_ip_{ij}$ is the long-run frequency of transitions $i\to j$ per unit time. The second is the product of "how often you are at $i$" and "how often you leave $i$ for $j$ when you are there".

Step 1 (part a). The balance equation for state 2 is $\pi_2=\sum_k\pi_kp_{k2}$: $$0.5(0.20)+0.3(0.50)+0.2(0.25)=0.10+0.15+0.05=0.30=\pi_2\;\checkmark .$$ (For completeness, state 1: $0.5(0.60)+0.3(0.40)+0.2(0.40)=0.30+0.12+0.08=0.50\;\checkmark$; state 3: $0.5(0.20)+0.3(0.10)+0.2(0.35)=0.10+0.03+0.07=0.20\;\checkmark$.)

Step 2 (part b). Expected number of visits to state 1 in $1000$ steps $\approx 1000\,\pi_1=1000(0.5)=500$. Simulation over $2{,}000{,}000$ steps gives an empirical fraction $0.5006\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part c). The long-run frequency of $2\to3$ transitions is $$\pi_2\,p_{23}=(0.3)(0.10)=0.03\ \text{per step},$$ so in $1000$ steps the expected count is $1000(0.03)=30$. Simulation gives an empirical rate $0.0296\;\checkmark$. In words: you are in state 2 about $300$ of the $1000$ steps, and on about $10\%$ of those you jump to 3.

Step 4 (part d). The mean recurrence time of state 3 is $$t_3^{*}=\frac{1}{\pi_3}=\frac{1}{0.2}=5\ \text{steps}.$$ (Likewise $t_1^{*}=1/0.5=2$.) Interpretation: visits to state 3 are spaced 5 steps apart on average, which is another way of saying the chain is in state 3 one step in five.

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.10+0.15+0.05=0.30=\pi_2\;\checkmark$; (b) $\approx500$ steps; (c) $\approx30$ transitions; (d) $t_3^{*}=5$ steps.

Trap. Answering $1000\,p_{23}=100$ in part (c). $p_{23}=0.10$ is a conditional frequency — the fraction of the visits to state 2 that go on to 3 — not a fraction of all time steps. You must weight it by how often you are at 2, giving $\pi_2p_{23}=0.03$ and hence $30$, not $100$.

Tool: G5 §3.3 (frequency interpretation of $\pi_j$ and of $\pi_ip_{ij}$), §4.4 (mean recurrence time) · B&T §7.3

Q107. ★★ · Spot the error: local balance · G5 §3.4

A chain on $\{0,1,2,3\}$ has $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.4&0.4&0.2&0\\0.3&0.4&0.3&0\\0&0.5&0.2&0.3\\0&0&0.6&0.4\end{pmatrix}.$$ A student writes "this is a birth–death chain, so I can use local balance": $\pi_0p_{01}=\pi_1p_{10}$, i.e. $0.4\pi_0=0.3\pi_1$, giving $\pi_1=\frac43\pi_0$. (a) Identify the error. (b) Write the correct cut equation across the boundary separating $\{0\}$ from $\{1,2,3\}$ and deduce the true ratio $\pi_1/\pi_0$. (c) The true steady state is $\pi=(5/27,\,10/27,\,8/27,\,4/27)$; verify it satisfies the cut equation separating $\{0,1\}$ from $\{2,3\}$.

Solution

Setup. Cut (or "flow balance") equations are always valid for any partition of the state space: in steady state, the long-run rate of transitions from $S$ to $S^{c}$ equals the rate from $S^{c}$ to $S$, because every crossing one way must eventually be matched by a crossing back. The local balance shortcut $\pi_ip_{i,i+1}=\pi_{i+1}p_{i+1,i}$ is the special case where the only way across the cut $\{0,\dots,i\}\mid\{i+1,\dots\}$ is the single pair of arrows $i\leftrightarrow i+1$ — i.e. a genuine birth–death chain.

Step 1 (part a — the error). This chain is not birth–death: $p_{02}=0.2>0$ is a jump of two states at once. So the cut between $\{0\}$ and $\{1,2,3\}$ is crossed not only by $0\to1$ but also by $0\to2$, and the student's equation leaves out that flow. The chain also lacks the return arrow $2\to0$, so the two sides of his equation are not even the same cut.

Step 2 (part b — the correct cut). Flow out of $\{0\}$: $\pi_0(p_{01}+p_{02})=\pi_0(0.4+0.2)=0.6\,\pi_0$. Flow into $\{0\}$: $\pi_1p_{10}+\pi_2p_{20}+\pi_3p_{30}=0.3\,\pi_1+0+0=0.3\,\pi_1$. Equating, $$0.6\,\pi_0=0.3\,\pi_1\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_1=2\,\pi_0 .$$ The true ratio is $2$, not the student's $4/3=1.333333$ — a $50\%$ error, and it propagates. Carrying his pairwise ratios all the way up and normalizing gives $\pi^{\text{student}}=(0.283019,\,0.377358,\,0.226415,\,0.113208)$ against the truth $(0.185185,\,0.370370,\,0.296296,\,0.148148)$: he overstates $\pi_0$ by $0.097834$, because he has ignored an escape route out of state 0 and so made state 0 look stickier than it is.

Step 3 (part c — verify the other cut). Across $\{0,1\}\mid\{2,3\}$ the crossing arrows are $0\to2$ ($0.2$), $1\to2$ ($0.3$) going out, and $2\to1$ ($0.5$) coming back ($p_{30}=p_{31}=0$). With $\pi_0=5/27=0.185185$, $\pi_1=10/27=0.370370$, $\pi_2=8/27=0.296296$: $$\text{out}=\pi_0(0.2)+\pi_1(0.3)=0.037037+0.111111=0.148148,$$ $$\text{in}=\pi_2(0.5)=(0.296296)(0.5)=0.148148\;\checkmark .$$ The two match exactly, confirming both $\pi$ and the principle: cut equations hold in general, pairwise local balance does not.

Answer. $\;$(a) the chain is not birth–death ($p_{02}=0.2$), so local balance does not apply; (b) $0.6\pi_0=0.3\pi_1$, hence $\pi_1=2\pi_0$; (c) both sides of the $\{0,1\}\mid\{2,3\}$ cut equal $0.148148\;\checkmark$.

Trap. Concluding "cut equations are unreliable here, use the full balance equations". Cut equations are perfectly reliable — what fails is the assumption that a cut is crossed by exactly one arrow in each direction. Write the cut with all crossing arrows and it works on any chain.

Tool: G5 §3.4 (birth–death chains, cut/local balance equations) · B&T §7.3

Q108. ★★★ · Doubly stochastic chains · G5 §3.1–3.2

Call an $m\times m$ transition matrix doubly stochastic if its columns as well as its rows sum to 1. (a) Prove that the uniform vector $\pi_j=1/m$ satisfies the balance equations for any doubly stochastic $P$. (b) Apply this to $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.2&0.3&0\\0.1&0.4&0.2&0.3\\0.2&0.3&0.1&0.4\\0.2&0.1&0.4&0.3\end{pmatrix}$$ and state the mean recurrence time of each state. (c) What additional hypotheses are needed before you may conclude $r_{ij}(n)\to1/m$, and give a doubly stochastic chain where that conclusion fails?

Solution

Setup. The balance equations are $\pi_j=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\pi_kp_{kj}$ for each $j$, together with $\sum_j\pi_j=1$. "Column $j$ sums to 1" says $\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{kj}=1$.

Step 1 (part a — the proof). Substitute the candidate $\pi_k=1/m$ into the right-hand side of the balance equation for an arbitrary $j$: $$\sum_{k=1}^{m}\pi_kp_{kj}=\sum_{k=1}^{m}\frac1m\,p_{kj}=\frac1m\sum_{k=1}^{m}p_{kj}=\frac1m\cdot1=\frac1m=\pi_j\;\checkmark,$$ where the third equality is exactly the doubly stochastic hypothesis. Normalization holds too: $\sum_{j}1/m=1\;\checkmark$. So the uniform distribution is always a stationary distribution of a doubly stochastic chain. $\blacksquare$

Step 2 (part b — check the hypothesis). Row sums: $0.5+0.2+0.3+0=1$; $0.1+0.4+0.2+0.3=1$; $0.2+0.3+0.1+0.4=1$; $0.2+0.1+0.4+0.3=1\;\checkmark$. Column sums: $0.5+0.1+0.2+0.2=1$; $0.2+0.4+0.3+0.1=1$; $0.3+0.2+0.1+0.4=1$; $0+0.3+0.4+0.3=1\;\checkmark$. Hence $\pi=(0.25,\,0.25,\,0.25,\,0.25)$. Every entry of $P^{50}$ equals $0.25$ to ten decimals $\checkmark$, and a simulation of $200{,}000$ chains gives $(0.2485,\,0.2519,\,0.2497,\,0.2500)\;\checkmark$. Mean recurrence times: $t_i^{*}=1/\pi_i=1/0.25=4$ steps for every state.

Step 3 (part c — from stationary to limiting). Part (a) only shows uniform is a stationary distribution. To conclude $r_{ij}(n)\to1/m$ for every $i$ you need the steady-state convergence theorem: a single recurrent class (which also makes $\pi$ unique) and aperiodicity. The chain in part (b) has all entries of $P^2$ positive, so it satisfies both. A counterexample: $p_{12}=p_{21}=1$ on two states is doubly stochastic (each column sums to $0+1=1$) and its unique stationary distribution is $(0.5,0.5)$, but $r_{11}(n)$ alternates $1,0,1,0,\dots$ and never converges. A second kind of failure is the identity matrix $P=I$, doubly stochastic with $m$ separate recurrent classes: uniform is stationary but $r_{ij}(n)=\delta_{ij}$ for all $n$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $\sum_k(1/m)p_{kj}=(1/m)\sum_kp_{kj}=1/m$; (b) $\pi=(0.25,0.25,0.25,0.25)$, $t_i^{*}=4$ for all $i$; (c) need a single, aperiodic recurrent class — e.g. $p_{12}=p_{21}=1$ is doubly stochastic yet $r_{11}(n)$ oscillates.

Trap. Concluding from part (a) that "doubly stochastic $\Rightarrow$ the long-run distribution is uniform whatever the start". The identity matrix refutes it: the chain simply stays where it began. Stationarity of the uniform vector is an algebraic fact; convergence to it is a dynamical one requiring the classification hypotheses.

Tool: G5 §3.1–3.2 (balance equations), §2.4 (convergence theorem) · B&T §7.3

Q109. ★ · Birth–death chain by local balance · G5 §3.4

A dock holds $0,1,2$ or $3$ kayaks. Over one hour the count goes up by one with probability $p_i$ and down by one with probability $q_i$, otherwise stays put, where $$p_0=0.5,\;p_1=0.4,\;p_2=0.3;\qquad q_1=0.6,\;q_2=0.5,\;q_3=0.7,$$ so that the transition matrix is $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.5&0.5&0&0\\0.6&0&0.4&0\\0&0.5&0.2&0.3\\0&0&0.7&0.3\end{pmatrix}.$$ Find the steady-state distribution using local balance, and the long-run average number of kayaks at the dock.

Solution

Setup. The chain moves only to neighboring states, so it is a birth–death chain and the cut between $\{0,\dots,i\}$ and $\{i+1,\dots,3\}$ is crossed by exactly one arrow each way. Local balance therefore applies: $\pi_ip_i=\pi_{i+1}q_{i+1}$ for $i=0,1,2$.

Step 1 (the three ratios). $$\pi_1=\frac{p_0}{q_1}\pi_0=\frac{0.5}{0.6}\pi_0=\frac56\pi_0,\qquad \pi_2=\frac{p_1}{q_2}\pi_1=\frac{0.4}{0.5}\cdot\frac56\pi_0=\frac45\cdot\frac56\pi_0=\frac23\pi_0,$$ $$\pi_3=\frac{p_2}{q_3}\pi_2=\frac{0.3}{0.7}\cdot\frac23\pi_0=\frac37\cdot\frac23\pi_0=\frac27\pi_0 .$$

Step 2 (normalize). Put everything over 42: $$1+\tfrac56+\tfrac23+\tfrac27=\tfrac{42}{42}+\tfrac{35}{42}+\tfrac{28}{42}+\tfrac{12}{42}=\tfrac{117}{42}=\tfrac{39}{14}=2.785714 .$$ Hence $\pi_0=\dfrac{42}{117}=\dfrac{14}{39}=0.358974$, and $$\pi_1=\tfrac{35}{117}=0.299145,\qquad \pi_2=\tfrac{28}{117}=0.239316,\qquad \pi_3=\tfrac{12}{117}=\tfrac{4}{39}=0.102564 .$$ Sum: $\frac{42+35+28+12}{117}=\frac{117}{117}=1\;\checkmark$. Solving the full $4\times4$ balance system numerically reproduces the same vector, and a simulation of $200{,}000$ chains gives $(0.3582,\,0.2969,\,0.2416,\,0.1033)\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (the average). $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i=0}^{3}i\,\pi_i=0+1(0.299145)+2(0.239316)+3(0.102564)=0.299145+0.478632+0.307692=1.085470 .$$

Answer. $\;\pi=(14/39,\,35/117,\,28/117,\,4/39)=(0.358974,\,0.299145,\,0.239316,\,0.102564)$; average $1.085470$ kayaks.

Trap. Using the self-transition probabilities in the local balance equations. Only the crossing arrows count: $p_{22}=0.2$ and $p_{33}=0.3$ never appear, because a step that stays in place crosses no cut. They are already accounted for by the rows summing to 1.

Tool: G5 §3.4 (birth–death chains and local balance) · B&T §7.3

Q110. ★★ · A queue and its load factor · G5 §3.5

Tickets arrive at a help desk in one-minute slots. In each slot a new ticket arrives with probability $p=0.2$ and, independently, the agent finishes one with probability $q=0.5$ — provided a ticket is present to be finished. The system holds at most $4$ tickets; an arrival finding $4$ present is turned away. Let $X_n$ be the number in the system at the end of slot $n$. In the interior, moving up requires an arrival and no departure and moving down requires a departure and no arrival, so for $i=1,2,3$ $$p_{i,i+1}=p(1-q)=0.1,\qquad p_{i,i-1}=q(1-p)=0.4 .$$ At the boundaries the mechanism changes: from $0$ no departure is possible, so $p_{01}=p=0.2$; at $4$ an arrival is blocked and lost, so $p_{43}=q=0.5$. (a) Compute the interior load factor $\rho=p_{i,i+1}/p_{i,i-1}$. (b) Find $\pi_0,\dots,\pi_4$ by local balance. (c) Find $\mathbb{E}[X]$. (d) Redo the calculation for an unlimited buffer.

Solution

Setup. Every transition is to a neighbor, so this is a birth–death chain and local balance applies at every cut: $\pi_i\,p_{i,i+1}=\pi_{i+1}\,p_{i+1,i}$. The full matrix, with the self-transition probabilities filling each row to 1, is $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.8&0.2&0&0&0\\0.4&0.5&0.1&0&0\\0&0.4&0.5&0.1&0\\0&0&0.4&0.5&0.1\\0&0&0&0.5&0.5\end{pmatrix},$$ row sums all $1\;\checkmark$.

Step 1 (part a). $$\rho=\frac{p(1-q)}{q(1-p)}=\frac{(0.2)(0.5)}{(0.5)(0.8)}=\frac{0.1}{0.4}=0.25 .$$ Since $\rho<1$ the queue is stable: in the interior, going down is four times as likely as going up, so the distribution decays geometrically by a factor $0.25$ per ticket.

Step 2 (part b — the four cut equations). The boundary cuts do not use $\rho$: $$\pi_0(0.2)=\pi_1(0.4)\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_1=\tfrac{0.2}{0.4}\pi_0=0.5\,\pi_0,$$ $$\pi_1(0.1)=\pi_2(0.4)\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_2=0.25\,\pi_1=0.125\,\pi_0,$$ $$\pi_2(0.1)=\pi_3(0.4)\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_3=0.25\,\pi_2=0.03125\,\pi_0,$$ $$\pi_3(0.1)=\pi_4(0.5)\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_4=0.2\,\pi_3=0.00625\,\pi_0 .$$

Step 3 (normalize). The relative weights are $1,\;0.5,\;0.125,\;0.03125,\;0.00625$, summing to $1.6625$. Hence $\pi_0=1/1.6625=0.601504$ and $$\pi=(0.601504,\;0.300752,\;0.075188,\;0.018797,\;0.003759).$$ Solving the full $5\times5$ balance system numerically reproduces this exactly, and $200{,}000$ simulated chains give $(0.6022,\,0.3005,\,0.0745,\,0.0192,\,0.0037)\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). $$\mathbb{E}[X]=\sum_{i=0}^{4}i\,\pi_i=0.300752+2(0.075188)+3(0.018797)+4(0.003759)$$ $$=0.300752+0.150376+0.056391+0.015038=0.522556\ \text{tickets}.$$

Step 5 (part d — unlimited buffer). Now $\pi_i=0.5\,\rho^{\,i-1}\pi_0$ for every $i\ge1$, so $$\frac{1}{\pi_0}=1+0.5\sum_{i\ge1}\rho^{\,i-1}=1+\frac{0.5}{1-0.25}=1+\frac{0.5}{0.75}=1.666667\;\Longrightarrow\;\pi_0=0.6 .$$ For the mean, use $\sum_{i\ge1}i\,r^{\,i-1}=1/(1-r)^2$ with $r=\rho=0.25$: $$\mathbb{E}[X]=0.6(0.5)\sum_{i\ge1}i\,\rho^{\,i-1}=\frac{0.3}{(0.75)^2}=\frac{0.3}{0.5625}=\frac{8}{15}=0.533333 ,$$ matching a direct 400-term summation $\checkmark$. So capping the buffer at 4 costs almost nothing: $0.522556$ versus $0.533333$, because $\pi_4=0.003759$ is already tiny. Truncation only bites when $\rho\to1$, where $\mathbb{E}[X]$ diverges and the buffer becomes the binding constraint.

Answer. $\;\rho=0.25$; $\pi=(0.601504,\,0.300752,\,0.075188,\,0.018797,\,0.003759)$; $\mathbb{E}[X]=0.522556$; with an unlimited buffer $\pi_0=0.6$ and $\mathbb{E}[X]=8/15=0.533333$.

Trap. Writing $\pi_i=\pi_0\rho^{\,i}$ straight down from $i=0$. That ignores the boundary row: at the empty state there is no departure to cancel the arrival, so the up-probability there is $p=0.2$, not $p(1-q)=0.1$, and the first ratio is $0.5$, not $0.25$. The pure-geometric shortcut returns $\pi_0=0.750733$ and $\mathbb{E}[X]=0.328446$ — a $37\%$ underestimate of the queue length. A second trap is $\rho=p/q=0.4$: it forgets that an arrival and a departure in the same slot cancel.

Tool: G5 §3.5 (the queue, load factor $\rho$), §3.4 (local balance) · B&T §7.3

Q111. ★★ · Which system of equations? · G5 §5.1

A chain on $\{1,2,3,4,5\}$ has $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.2&0.5&0.1&0.2&0\\0.3&0.1&0.4&0&0.2\\0&0.4&0.2&0.1&0.3\\0&0&0&1&0\\0&0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$ For each question below, name the system of equations you would set up, write its unknowns and boundary conditions, and give the answer. (i) $\mathbf{P}(X_3=2\mid X_0=1)$. (ii) Starting from 1, the probability of ending up absorbed in state 4. (iii) Starting from 1, the expected number of steps until absorption. (iv) The long-run fraction of time spent in state 2.

Solution

Setup. States 4 and 5 are absorbing; $\{1,2,3\}$ is a transient class, since from 1 you can reach 4 and never return. Each of the four questions is a different linear system — recognizing which is the entire skill.

Step 1 (i) — $n$-step recursion. Unknowns $r_{ij}(n)$; recursion $r_{ij}(n)=\sum_kr_{ik}(n-1)p_{kj}$ with initial condition $r_{ij}(0)=\delta_{ij}$. No boundary conditions and no solving — just iterate (equivalently, read off $P^3$). Iterating gives $$\mathbf{P}(X_3=2\mid X_0=1)=0.21 ,$$ confirmed by $400{,}000$ simulated paths ($0.2100$).

Step 2 (ii) — absorption equations. Unknowns $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }4\mid X_0=i)$ for the transient $i$; equations $a_i=\sum_{j}p_{ij}a_j$ with the boundary conditions $a_4=1$, $a_5=0$. That is $$a_1=0.2a_1+0.5a_2+0.1a_3+0.2,\quad a_2=0.3a_1+0.1a_2+0.4a_3,\quad a_3=0.4a_2+0.2a_3+0.1 .$$ Solving the $3\times3$ system: $(a_1,a_2,a_3)=(0.446203,\,0.262658,\,0.256329)$, so the answer is $a_1=0.446203$. Simulation gives $0.4461\;\checkmark$. Note there is no "$1+$" here — these are probabilities, and time does not enter.

Step 3 (iii) — expected-time equations. Unknowns $\mu_i=\mathbb{E}[\text{steps to absorption}\mid X_0=i]$; equations $\mu_i=1+\sum_{j\text{ transient}}p_{ij}\mu_j$ with $\mu_4=\mu_5=0$. The "$1+$" counts the step you are about to take. Solving gives $$(\mu_1,\mu_2,\mu_3)=(4.082278,\,3.892405,\,3.196203),$$ so the answer is $\mu_1=4.082278$ steps. Same coefficient matrix $I-Q$ as in (ii), different right-hand side (all ones instead of the absorption column).

Step 4 (iv) — balance equations, and a warning. The relevant system is $\pi_j=\sum_k\pi_kp_{kj}$, $\sum_j\pi_j=1$. But state 2 is transient, so it is visited only finitely often and $\pi_2=0$: the long-run fraction of time in state 2 is $0$. Moreover this chain has two recurrent classes $\{4\}$ and $\{5\}$, so the balance system does not have a unique solution — every vector $(0,0,0,\alpha,1-\alpha)$ satisfies it. The correct long-run answer conditional on the start is $(0,0,0,0.446203,\,0.553797)$ from state 1, which is exactly the absorption probabilities from Step 2. This is the sense in which question (iv) is really question (ii) in disguise.

Answer. $\;$(i) $n$-step recursion, $0.21$; (ii) absorption equations with $a_4=1,a_5=0$, $a_1=0.446203$; (iii) expected-time equations with the $1+$ and $\mu_4=\mu_5=0$, $\mu_1=4.082278$; (iv) balance equations — but $\pi_2=0$ because 2 is transient, and the balance system is not uniquely solvable here.

Trap. Setting up the absorption system with a "$1+$" (that is question iii) or the time system without it (that would give $\mu_i=0$). A second trap is expecting a nonzero $\pi_2$: a chain with absorbing states has all its steady-state mass on the absorbing states, so "what fraction of time in state 2?" always answers $0$, however long it lingers there first.

Tool: G5 §5.1 (the three Markov question types), §1.3, §3.1, §4.1–4.2 · B&T §7.1, §7.3–7.4

Q112. ★ · Absorption probability by first-step analysis · G5 §4.1

A token sits on one of four squares $1,2,3,4$. Squares 1 and 4 end the game. From square 2 the token moves to 1 with probability $0.3$ and to 3 with probability $0.7$; from square 3 it moves to 2 with probability $0.5$ and to 4 with probability $0.5$. Starting on square 2, what is the probability that the game ends on square 4?

Solution

Setup. Let $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }4\mid X_0=i)$. The boundary conditions are $a_4=1$ and $a_1=0$. First-step analysis conditions on the first move and uses the Markov property to restart the same problem from the new state: $a_i=\sum_jp_{ij}a_j$ for the transient $i=2,3$.

Step 1 (the two equations). $$a_2=0.3\,a_1+0.7\,a_3=0.3(0)+0.7a_3=0.7\,a_3,$$ $$a_3=0.5\,a_2+0.5\,a_4=0.5\,a_2+0.5(1)=0.5\,a_2+0.5 .$$

Step 2 (substitute). Put the first into the second: $$a_3=0.5(0.7a_3)+0.5=0.35\,a_3+0.5\;\Longrightarrow\;0.65\,a_3=0.5\;\Longrightarrow\;a_3=\frac{0.5}{0.65}=\frac{10}{13}=0.769231 .$$

Step 3 (back-substitute). $$a_2=0.7\,a_3=0.7\cdot\frac{10}{13}=\frac{7}{13}=0.538462 .$$ Check the first equation directly: $0.3(0)+0.7(0.769231)=0.538462\;\checkmark$. Monte Carlo with $300{,}000$ games gives $0.5382$ from square 2 and $0.7689$ from square 3 $\checkmark$.

Answer. $\;a_2=7/13=0.538462$ (so the game ends on square 1 with probability $6/13=0.461538$).

Trap. Guessing $0.5$ "by symmetry" because square 2 sits between the two ends. The chain is not symmetric: from 2 the token heads toward 4 with probability $0.7$ but toward 1 with only $0.3$, while square 3 is an even coin flip. That bias is what pushes $a_2$ above $0.5$.

Tool: G5 §4.1 (absorption probabilities, first-step analysis) · B&T §7.4

Q113. ★★ · Expected time to absorption · G5 §4.2

A beetle wanders among three chambers $1,2,3$ and an exit, state $4$, moving once per minute according to $$P=\begin{pmatrix}0.4&0.6&0&0\\0.3&0.2&0.5&0\\0&0.6&0&0.4\\0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$ Starting in chamber 1, how long on average until the beetle exits? Give $\mu_1,\mu_2,\mu_3$.

Solution

Setup. Let $\mu_i=\mathbb{E}[\text{minutes until state 4}\mid X_0=i]$, with $\mu_4=0$. First-step analysis: take one step (costing 1 minute), then face the same problem from wherever you land: $$\mu_i=1+\sum_{j=1}^{4}p_{ij}\,\mu_j,\qquad i=1,2,3 .$$

Step 1 (write the three equations). $$\mu_1=1+0.4\mu_1+0.6\mu_2,\qquad \mu_2=1+0.3\mu_1+0.2\mu_2+0.5\mu_3,\qquad \mu_3=1+0.6\mu_2+0.4(0)=1+0.6\mu_2 .$$

Step 2 (eliminate $\mu_1$). From the first, $0.6\mu_1=1+0.6\mu_2$, so $$\mu_1=\frac{1}{0.6}+\mu_2=\frac53+\mu_2=1.666667+\mu_2 .$$

Step 3 (substitute both into the middle equation). $$\mu_2=1+0.3\left(\tfrac53+\mu_2\right)+0.2\mu_2+0.5\left(1+0.6\mu_2\right)$$ $$=1+0.5+0.3\mu_2+0.2\mu_2+0.5+0.3\mu_2=2+0.8\,\mu_2 .$$ Hence $0.2\,\mu_2=2$ and $\mu_2=10$.

Step 4 (back-substitute). $$\mu_3=1+0.6(10)=7,\qquad \mu_1=\tfrac53+10=\tfrac{35}{3}=11.666667 .$$ Check the middle equation: $1+0.3(11.666667)+0.2(10)+0.5(7)=1+3.5+2+3.5=10=\mu_2\;\checkmark$. A Monte-Carlo run of $200{,}000$ beetles started in chamber 1 gives a mean exit time of $11.6482$ minutes $\checkmark$.
