This gives $n\ge(2.5758293\sqrt{8.3333}/0.5)^2=222$, and it is not valid: it is an assumption about the shape of the distribution, and the model said only that the scores lie in $[0,10]$. If the scores are in fact bimodal — many $0$'s and many $10$'s, which is common for pass/fail-like scoring — the true variance is up to $25$, three times the assumed value, and the delivered half-width would be $2.5758293\sqrt{25/222}=0.8643917$, not $0.5$. The interval would then be a $99\%$ interval in name only. This is the difference between a bound and a guess.

Answer. $\;$(a) $\operatorname{var}(X)\le25$, attained by the two-point law $\tfrac12\delta_0+\tfrac12\delta_{10}$; (b) $n=664$; (c) $\sigma=10$ gives $n=2654$ (valid but $4\times$ wasteful), the uniform assumption gives $n=222$ (invalid — an unjustified distributional assumption, not a bound).

Trap. Reaching for the Bernoulli $\tfrac14$ bound directly. That bound is $\operatorname{var}\le\tfrac14$ for a variable on $[0,1]$; the general statement is $\operatorname{var}\le(b-a)^2/4$ for support $[a,b]$, and forgetting to rescale by $(b-a)^2=100$ would give $n=\lceil(2.5758293\times0.5/0.5)^2\rceil=7$ — off by two orders of magnitude.

Tool: G7 §1.4 (CI for a mean), §1.6 (bounding an unknown variance; the $\theta(1-\theta)\le1/4$ device) · B&T §9.1

Q141. ★ · Bernoulli ML and invariance · G7 §2.2

A machine either passes or fails a self-test each morning, independently, with unknown failure probability $\theta$. Over $n=40$ mornings it failed $13$ times. (a) Write the likelihood and derive the ML estimate $\hat\theta$ from scratch. (b) Report the ML estimate of the odds $\theta/(1-\theta)$ and of the probability $\theta^2$ that it fails on two specified consecutive mornings, naming the principle you used. (c) How many times more likely are the observed data under $\hat\theta$ than under $\theta=1/2$?

Solution

Setup. Let $X_1,\dots,X_{40}$ be i.i.d. Bernoulli($\theta$), $X_i=1$ for a failure, and let $k=\sum_i x_i=13$ be the observed number of failures.

Step 1 — the likelihood. By independence the joint PMF factors: $$L(\theta)=p_X(x;\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^{40}\theta^{x_i}(1-\theta)^{1-x_i} =\theta^{k}(1-\theta)^{n-k}=\theta^{13}(1-\theta)^{27}.$$ (This is a function of $\theta$ with the data frozen — not a probability distribution over $\theta$; see the §2.1 gotcha.)

Step 2 — log and differentiate. The logarithm is increasing, so it has the same maximizer, and it turns the product into a sum: $$\ell(\theta)=13\log\theta+27\log(1-\theta),\qquad \frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=\frac{13}{\theta}-\frac{27}{1-\theta}.$$ Setting this to zero and multiplying through by $\theta(1-\theta)>0$: $$13(1-\theta)-27\theta=0\;\Longrightarrow\;13=40\theta\;\Longrightarrow\; \hat\theta=\frac{13}{40}=0.325 .$$ It is a maximum: $\ell''(\theta)=-13/\theta^2-27/(1-\theta)^2<0$ everywhere, so $\ell$ is strictly concave and the stationary point is the global maximum. In general $\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}=k/n$ — the sample frequency.

Step 3 — invariance. ML estimates transform: if $\hat\theta$ maximizes the likelihood in $\theta$ and $g$ is any function, the ML estimate of $g(\theta)$ is $g(\hat\theta)$ (G7 §2.7, the invariance principle). Hence $$\widehat{\text{odds}}=\frac{0.325}{1-0.325}=\frac{0.325}{0.675}=0.4814815, \qquad \widehat{\theta^2}=(0.325)^2=0.105625 .$$ No new maximization is needed.

Step 4 — likelihood ratio against $\theta=1/2$. $$\ell(\hat\theta)=13\log(0.325)+27\log(0.675)=-25.2232411,\qquad \ell(0.5)=40\log(0.5)=-27.7258872 .$$ The difference is $2.5026461$, so $$\frac{L(\hat\theta)}{L(0.5)}=e^{2.5026461}=12.2148 .$$ The data are about $12.2$ times more likely under $\hat\theta=0.325$ than under a fair-coin machine.

Answer. $\;\hat\theta=0.325$; $\widehat{\text{odds}}=0.4814815$; $\widehat{\theta^2}=0.105625$; likelihood ratio $=12.2148$.

Trap. Reading the $12.2$ as "the machine is $12.2$ times more likely to have $\theta=0.325$ than $\theta=0.5$." Likelihoods are probabilities of the data, not of the parameter; converting them into statements about $\theta$ requires a prior (G6 §3). A second trap: estimating the odds by rerunning the maximization in a new parametrization — unnecessary, and invariance guarantees the same answer.

Tool: G7 §2.1–2.2 (likelihood, log-likelihood, the smooth case), §2.7 (invariance) · B&T §9.1

Q142. ★★ · Normal ML, both parameters · G7 §2.3

Five independent readings of a reference voltage, modeled as $N(\mu,v)$ with both $\mu$ and $v$ unknown, are $$9.8,\quad 10.4,\quad 9.1,\quad 10.9,\quad 10.3 .$$ (a) Report $\hat\mu_{\text{ML}}$ and $\hat v_{\text{ML}}$, showing the arithmetic. (b) Compare $\hat v_{\text{ML}}$ with the unbiased sample variance $\hat s_n^2$ and state the exact relation between the two. (c) Give the ML estimate of $\mathbf{P}(X>11)$, i.e. the chance a future reading exceeds $11$ volts.

Solution

Setup. From G7 §2.3, maximizing $\ell(\mu,v)=-\frac n2\log(2\pi v)-\frac{1}{2v}\sum_i(x_i-\mu)^2$ gives $$\hat\mu_{\text{ML}}=\frac1n\sum_i x_i=m_n,\qquad \hat v_{\text{ML}}=\frac1n\sum_i(x_i-m_n)^2 .$$ The $\mu$-equation does not involve $v$, so the two maximizations decouple: solve for $\mu$ first, then substitute. Here $n=5$.

Step 1 — the mean. $$\sum_i x_i=9.8+10.4+9.1+10.9+10.3=50.5,\qquad \hat\mu_{\text{ML}}=\frac{50.5}{5}=10.1 .$$

Step 2 — the variance. Deviations from $10.1$: $$-0.3,\quad 0.3,\quad -1.0,\quad 0.8,\quad 0.2 ,$$ whose squares are $0.09,\ 0.09,\ 1.00,\ 0.64,\ 0.04$, summing to $$\sum_i(x_i-10.1)^2=1.86 .$$ Hence $$\hat v_{\text{ML}}=\frac{1.86}{5}=0.372 .$$

Step 3 — the unbiased comparison. $$\hat s_n^2=\frac{1}{n-1}\sum_i(x_i-m_n)^2=\frac{1.86}{4}=0.465,\qquad \frac{\hat s_n^2}{\hat v_{\text{ML}}}=\frac{0.465}{0.372}=1.25=\frac{n}{n-1}.$$ Exactly, $\hat v_{\text{ML}}=\frac{n-1}{n}\hat s_n^2$, so $\mathbb{E}[\hat v_{\text{ML}}]=\frac{n-1}{n}v=0.8v$: the ML variance is biased downward by $20\%$ at $n=5$. (Simulating $300{,}000$ samples of size $5$ from $N(0,4)$ gives $\mathbb{E}[\hat v_{\text{ML}}]/v=0.79997$. $\checkmark$) The reason is structural: the deviations are taken from $m_n$, the point that minimizes the sum of squares, so the sum is systematically too small; dividing by $n-1$ instead of $n$ repairs it exactly.

Step 4 — the tail probability, by invariance. $\mathbf{P}(X>11)$ is a function of $(\mu,v)$, so its ML estimate is that function evaluated at the ML estimates (G7 §2.7). With $\hat\sigma=\sqrt{0.372}=0.6099180$, $$z=\frac{11-10.1}{0.6099180}=\frac{0.9}{0.6099180}=1.4756081,\qquad \widehat{\mathbf{P}}(X>11)=1-\Phi(1.4756081)=0.0700246 .$$

Answer. $\;\hat\mu_{\text{ML}}=10.1$, $\hat v_{\text{ML}}=0.372$; $\hat s_n^2=0.465=\frac{n}{n-1}\hat v_{\text{ML}}$; $\widehat{\mathbf{P}}(X>11)=0.0700246$.

Trap. Using $\hat s_n^2=0.465$ in Step 4 "because it is the unbiased one." The invariance principle applies to the ML estimates; substituting $0.465$ gives $1-\Phi(0.9/0.6819091)=1-\Phi(1.3198240)=0.0934469$, a $33.4\%$ larger answer, and it is the ML estimate of nothing. (Note also that unbiasedness is not preserved by nonlinear maps: $\hat s_n$ is not an unbiased estimate of $\sigma$ even though $\hat s_n^2$ is unbiased for $v$.)

Tool: G7 §2.3 (normal ML for mean and variance), §2.7 (invariance), §1.6 ($\hat s_n^2$) · B&T Example 9.4, §9.1

Q143. ★★ · ML when calculus fails · G7 §2.4

Detector response times are modeled as i.i.d. shifted exponentials: for an unknown threshold $\theta$, $$f_X(x;\theta)=e^{-(x-\theta)}\ \ \text{for } x\ge\theta,\qquad f_X(x;\theta)=0\ \ \text{for } x<\theta .$$ Five times are recorded: $3.2,\ 4.7,\ 3.9,\ 5.1,\ 3.5$ (seconds). (a) Write the likelihood and explain why setting $d\ell/d\theta=0$ finds nothing. (b) Find $\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}$. (c) Given that $\min_i X_i-\theta$ is exponential with rate $n$, find the bias of $\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}$, build an unbiased estimator, and compare the two MSEs.

Solution

Setup. $n=5$, $\sum_i x_i=3.2+4.7+3.9+5.1+3.5=20.4$, and $\min_i x_i=3.2$. The parameter here is a boundary of the support, which is the signature of a corner solution (G7 §2.4).

Step 1 — the likelihood, with its constraint written down. By independence, $$L(\theta)=\prod_{i=1}^{5}f_X(x_i;\theta) =\begin{cases}\displaystyle e^{\,n\theta-\sum_i x_i}=e^{5\theta-20.4}, & \theta\le\min_i x_i=3.2,\\[4pt] 0, & \theta>3.2,\end{cases}$$ because a single observation below $\theta$ makes its density — and hence the whole product — zero. The constraint $\theta\le3.2$ is not decoration; it is the information the data carry about $\theta$.

Step 2 — why differentiation fails. On the region where $L>0$, $$\ell(\theta)=5\theta-20.4,\qquad \frac{d\ell}{d\theta}=5 .$$ The derivative is the constant $+5$: never zero, so there is no stationary point at all. The log-likelihood is strictly increasing in $\theta$ and then falls off a cliff to $-\infty$ at $\theta=3.2^+$. Calculus finds nothing because the maximum is not interior.

Step 3 — the ML estimate. Since $\ell$ increases on $(-\infty,3.2]$, push $\theta$ as far right as allowed: $$\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}=\min_i x_i=3.2 .$$ Sanity check on the values: $\ell(3.2)=5(3.2)-20.4=-4.4$, while $\ell(3.0)=-5.4$ and $\ell(2.0)=-10.4$ — the likelihood really is climbing right up to the boundary.

Step 4 — bias and correction. Let $Y=\min_i X_i-\theta$. We are given $Y\sim\text{Exp}(n)$, so $\mathbb{E}[Y]=1/n=1/5=0.2$ and $\operatorname{var}(Y)=1/n^2=0.04$. Therefore $$\mathbb{E}[\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}]=\theta+0.2,\qquad \mathrm{bias}=+0.2 ,$$ a systematic overestimate — unsurprising, since the smallest of five observations is still above the true floor. The obvious repair subtracts the bias: $$\tilde\Theta=\min_i X_i-\frac1n = 3.2-0.2=3.0,\qquad \mathbb{E}[\tilde\Theta]=\theta .$$ (A $400{,}000$-run simulation with $\theta=3$ gives $\mathbb{E}[\min_iX_i]-\theta=0.19971$ and $\operatorname{var}(\min_iX_i)=0.04001$. $\checkmark$)

Step 5 — the MSEs. Subtracting a constant does not change the variance, so both estimators have variance $1/n^2=0.04$, and $$\mathrm{MSE}(\hat\theta_{\text{ML}})=0.04+(0.2)^2=0.04+0.04=0.08,\qquad \mathrm{MSE}(\tilde\Theta)=0.04+0=0.04 .$$ Here the unbiased version halves the MSE — the opposite verdict from Q137, and for a clear reason: the correction there was a rescaling (which changes the variance too), whereas here it is a shift (which does not), so removing the bias is pure profit.

Answer. $\;\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}=3.2$; bias $=+1/n=0.2$; unbiased estimator $\min_iX_i-1/n=3.0$; MSEs $0.08$ and $0.04$ respectively.

Trap. Writing $\ell(\theta)=n\theta-\sum_ix_i$ without the constraint and concluding "no maximum, $\hat\theta=+\infty$." The constraint region is part of the likelihood; dropping it loses the entire answer. The mirror-image trap is answering $\bar x=20.4/5=4.08$ because "the mean of the model is $\theta+1$, so $\hat\theta=\bar x-1=3.08$" — that is the method-of-moments estimate, a legitimate but different estimator, and it is not the maximizer of $L$ (indeed $\theta=3.08$ is feasible here, but with a sample containing a value below $3.08$ it could even be impossible).

Tool: G7 §2.4 (ML when calculus fails: parameters in the support boundary; the uniform $[0,\theta]$ case) · B&T Example 9.1, Problem 9.9

Q144. ★★ · ML, MAP and the flat prior · G7 §5.2

A new coin is tossed $n=6$ times and lands heads $k=5$ times. Let $\theta$ be the probability of heads. (a) Give the ML estimate. (b) Give the MAP estimate under a uniform prior on $[0,1]$, and prove the general statement "MAP $=$ ML when the prior is flat". (c) Give the MAP estimate under the prior $f_\Theta(\theta)\propto\theta(1-\theta)$ on $[0,1]$. (d) Give the posterior mean under the uniform prior, using $\int_0^1\theta^a(1-\theta)^b\,d\theta=\frac{a!\,b!}{(a+b+1)!}$. (e) Which of these four numbers would you report, and what question does each answer?

Solution

Setup. The data enter only through the likelihood $L(\theta)=\binom{6}{5}\theta^5(1-\theta)^1$; the binomial coefficient is a constant in $\theta$ and never affects any maximizer.

(a) ML. By Q141's derivation, $\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}=k/n=5/6=0.8333333$.

(b) MAP under a flat prior. Bayes' rule gives the posterior $$f_{\Theta\mid X}(\theta\mid x)=\frac{f_\Theta(\theta)\,p_{X\mid\Theta}(x\mid\theta)} {p_X(x)} .$$ The denominator $p_X(x)$ does not depend on $\theta$. If the prior is flat, $f_\Theta(\theta)=1$ on $[0,1]$, it does not depend on $\theta$ either, so $$f_{\Theta\mid X}(\theta\mid x)\ \propto\ p_{X\mid\Theta}(x\mid\theta)=L(\theta) \quad\text{on }[0,1].$$ Two functions that are positive multiples of one another have the same maximizer, hence $\hat\theta_{\text{MAP}}=\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}$ — provided the ML estimate lies inside the prior's support, which it does here. Numerically, maximizing $\theta^5(1-\theta)$ on a $2{,}000{,}001$-point grid returns $0.8333335$. $\checkmark$ So $\hat\theta_{\text{MAP}}=5/6=0.8333333$.

(c) MAP under $f_\Theta(\theta)\propto\theta(1-\theta)$. Now the posterior is proportional to $$\theta(1-\theta)\cdot\theta^5(1-\theta)=\theta^6(1-\theta)^2 .$$ Take logs and differentiate: $\frac{6}{\theta}-\frac{2}{1-\theta}=0$ gives $6(1-\theta)=2\theta$, i.e. $6=8\theta$ and $$\hat\theta_{\text{MAP}}=\frac{6}{8}=0.75 .$$ (Grid check: $0.7500000$. $\checkmark$) The prior, which favors mid-range coins, has pulled the estimate from $0.8333$ toward $0.5$ — it acts like two extra pseudo-observations, one head and one tail.

(d) Posterior mean under the flat prior. $$\mathbb{E}[\Theta\mid X=x] =\frac{\int_0^1\theta\cdot\theta^5(1-\theta)\,d\theta}{\int_0^1\theta^5(1-\theta)\,d\theta} =\frac{\frac{6!\,1!}{8!}}{\frac{5!\,1!}{7!}} =\frac{720/40320}{120/5040}=\frac{0.0178571}{0.0238095}=0.75 .$$ In general this is $(k+1)/(n+2)=6/8=0.75$, Laplace's rule of succession. (Numerical integration returns $0.7500000$. $\checkmark$)

(e) What each number answers. $\hat\theta_{\text{ML}}=0.8333$: "which $\theta$ makes the observed data most likely?" — no prior, no probability statement about $\theta$. $\hat\theta_{\text{MAP}}$ (flat) $=0.8333$: "which $\theta$ has the highest posterior density?" — numerically identical here, but a genuinely different question, and it comes with a full posterior you can integrate. $\hat\theta_{\text{MAP}}$ (Beta-shaped prior) $=0.75$: the same question with real prior information supplied. Posterior mean $=0.75$: "what is the LMS estimate, minimizing $\mathbb{E}[(\Theta-\hat\theta)^2\mid X]$?" (G6 §4). With six tosses I would report $0.75$ with the posterior attached, since ML's $0.8333$ has an awkward feature — it would have returned $1.0$ had all six landed heads, asserting that tails is impossible on the evidence of six tosses.

Answer. $\;$ML $=5/6=0.8333333$; MAP (flat) $=5/6=0.8333333$; MAP (prior $\propto\theta(1-\theta)$) $=0.75$; posterior mean (flat) $=(k+1)/(n+2)=0.75$.

Trap. "A flat prior makes everything Bayesian collapse to the classical answer." Only the mode collapses. The posterior mean under the same flat prior is $0.75$, differing from ML by $0.0833$; the posterior variance, credible intervals and predictive distribution have no classical counterpart at all. A flat prior kills the difference between MAP and ML, not the difference between Bayesian and classical inference.

Tool: G7 §5.2 (Bayesian/classical master comparison; MAP $\to$ ML under a flat prior) · G6 §3 (MAP), §4 (LMS/posterior mean) · B&T §8.2, §9.1

Q145. ★ · Least squares from the closed forms · G7 §3.2–3.3

A pump is run at five settings $x$ (in units of $100$ rpm) and the delivered flow $y$ (liters/min) is recorded:

$$\begin{array}{c|ccccc} x_i & 1 & 2 & 3 & 4 & 5\\\hline y_i & 2.1 & 3.9 & 6.2 & 7.8 & 10.0 \end{array}$$

(a) Fit the least-squares line $y\approx\hat\theta_0+\hat\theta_1x$. (b) Report the residuals and verify the two identities every least-squares fit satisfies: $\sum_ie_i=0$ and $\sum_ix_ie_i=0$. (c) Compute $\mathrm{SSE}$ and $R^2$. (d) Predict the flow at $x=6$.

Solution

Setup. The closed forms of G7 §3.2 are $$\hat\theta_1=\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{xx}},\qquad \hat\theta_0=\bar y-\hat\theta_1\bar x,$$ with $S_{xx}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)^2$, $S_{xy}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(y_i-\bar y)$, $S_{yy}=\sum_i(y_i-\bar y)^2$. Here $n=5$.

Step 1 — the two means. $\sum_ix_i=1+2+3+4+5=15$ so $\bar x=15/5=3$; $\sum_iy_i=2.1+3.9+6.2+7.8+10.0=30.0$ so $\bar y=30/5=6.0$.

Step 2 — the three sums. Centered values: $x_i-\bar x=-2,-1,0,1,2$ and $y_i-\bar y=-3.9,-2.1,0.2,1.8,4.0$. $$S_{xx}=4+1+0+1+4=10,$$ $$S_{xy}=(-2)(-3.9)+(-1)(-2.1)+(0)(0.2)+(1)(1.8)+(2)(4.0) =7.8+2.1+0+1.8+8.0=19.7,$$ $$S_{yy}=15.21+4.41+0.04+3.24+16.00=38.90 .$$

Step 3 — the coefficients. $$\hat\theta_1=\frac{19.7}{10}=1.97,\qquad \hat\theta_0=6.0-1.97\times3=6.0-5.91=0.09 .$$ So the fit is $y\approx0.09+1.97x$. (A direct least-squares solve returns $\hat\theta_0=0.09$, $\hat\theta_1=1.97$. $\checkmark$) Reading it: each extra $100$ rpm buys about $1.97$ L/min of flow.

Step 4 — fitted values and residuals. $$\hat y = 2.06,\ 4.03,\ 6.00,\ 7.97,\ 9.94 \quad\Longrightarrow\quad e = 0.04,\ -0.13,\ 0.20,\ -0.17,\ 0.06 .$$ Check the identities: $$\sum_ie_i=0.04-0.13+0.20-0.17+0.06=0.00,$$ $$\sum_ix_ie_i=1(0.04)+2(-0.13)+3(0.20)+4(-0.17)+5(0.06) =0.04-0.26+0.60-0.68+0.30=0.00 .$$ Both vanish, as they must: they are the two normal equations $\partial Q/\partial\theta_0=0$ and $\partial Q/\partial\theta_1=0$ rewritten.

Step 5 — SSE and $R^2$. Directly, $$\mathrm{SSE}=0.04^2+0.13^2+0.20^2+0.17^2+0.06^2 =0.0016+0.0169+0.0400+0.0289+0.0036=0.0910 .$$ The shortcut of §3.3 agrees without forming residuals: $\mathrm{SSE}=S_{yy}-S_{xy}^2/S_{xx}=38.90-19.7^2/10=38.90-38.809=0.091$. $\checkmark$ Then $$R^2=1-\frac{\mathrm{SSE}}{S_{yy}}=1-\frac{0.091}{38.90}=1-0.0023393=0.9976607 .$$

Step 6 — prediction. $\hat y(6)=0.09+1.97\times6=0.09+11.82=11.91$ L/min.

Answer. $\;\hat\theta_0=0.09$, $\hat\theta_1=1.97$; residuals $(0.04,-0.13,0.20,-0.17,0.06)$ with both identities $=0$; $\mathrm{SSE}=0.091$, $R^2=0.9976607$; $\hat y(6)=11.91$.

Trap. Computing $\hat\theta_0$ as $\bar y - \hat\theta_1$ (forgetting $\bar x$), which gives $6.0-1.97=4.03$ — a line missing every data point. The intercept formula exists precisely to force the line through the centroid $(\bar x,\bar y)=(3,6)$, and indeed $\hat y(3)=0.09+5.91=6.00=\bar y$.

Tool: G7 §3.2 (normal equations and the closed forms), §3.3 (residual identities, $R^2$, the $\mathrm{SSE}=S_{yy}-S_{xy}^2/S_{xx}$ shortcut) · B&T §9.2

Q146. ★★ · Spot the error · G7 §3.2

Using Q145's data ($x=1,\dots,5$; $y=2.1,\ 3.9,\ 6.2,\ 7.8,\ 10.0$), a student writes $$\hat\theta_1=\frac{\sum_i x_iy_i}{\sum_i x_i^2}$$ "because that is the slope formula", computes $109.7/55=1.9945455$, and reports a fit $y\approx0.09+1.9945x$ (keeping Q145's intercept). (a) Identify the error precisely and give the correct identity relating $\sum_ix_iy_i$ to $S_{xy}$. (b) Is $1.9945455$ the least-squares slope of any model? If so, which one, and what is its SSE compared with the correct fit's $0.091$? (c) What is the single worst consequence of the student's reported line?

Solution

Setup. The raw sums are $\sum_ix_iy_i=2.1+7.8+18.6+31.2+50.0=109.7$, $\sum_ix_i^2=1+4+9+16+25=55$, $\sum_iy_i^2=4.41+15.21+38.44+60.84+100.00=218.9$, with $\bar x=3$, $\bar y=6$, $n=5$.

(a) The error: no centering. The correct closed form uses centered sums. The bookkeeping identity (expand the product and use $\sum_ix_i=n\bar x$) is $$S_{xy}=\sum_i(x_i-\bar x)(y_i-\bar y)=\sum_ix_iy_i-n\bar x\bar y =109.7-5(3)(6)=109.7-90=19.7,\ \checkmark$$ $$S_{xx}=\sum_ix_i^2-n\bar x^2=55-5(9)=55-45=10 .\ \checkmark$$ So the correct slope is $19.7/10=1.97$; the student computed $109.7/55=1.9945455$, which drops the $-n\bar x\bar y$ and $-n\bar x^2$ corrections. The discrepancy is $0.0245455$. The formula $\sum x_iy_i/\sum x_i^2$ is only equal to $S_{xy}/S_{xx}$ when $\bar x=0$ (or $\bar y=0$) — which is why the mistake is invisible on centered data and lethal otherwise.

(b) It is the right answer to a different question. $\sum x_iy_i/\sum x_i^2$ is exactly the least-squares slope for the no-intercept model $Y_i=\theta_1x_i+W_i$: minimizing $Q(\theta_1)=\sum_i(y_i-\theta_1x_i)^2$ gives $\frac{dQ}{d\theta_1}=-2\sum_ix_i(y_i-\theta_1x_i)=0$, i.e. $\theta_1\sum_ix_i^2=\sum_ix_iy_i$. Its SSE is $$\mathrm{SSE}_{\text{no-int}}=\sum_iy_i^2-\frac{(\sum_ix_iy_i)^2}{\sum_ix_i^2} =218.9-\frac{109.7^2}{55}=218.9-\frac{12034.09}{55}=218.9-218.8016=0.0983636 .$$ Against the two-parameter fit's $0.0910$, the excess is $0.0073636$ — the no-intercept model is worse, as it must be, since it is the two-parameter model constrained to $\theta_0=0$ and a constrained minimum cannot beat an unconstrained one.

(c) The worst consequence: an incoherent line. The student has spliced a slope from the no-intercept model onto an intercept from the with-intercept model. The resulting line $y=0.09+1.9945x$ minimizes nothing: it is not the least-squares fit of either model, its residuals do not satisfy $\sum_ie_i=0$ or $\sum_ix_ie_i=0$, and it no longer passes through the centroid $(3,6)$ — at $x=3$ it predicts $0.09+5.9836=6.0736$ instead of $6.00$. Every diagnostic downstream ($R^2$, the residual plot, any confidence interval for the slope) is then computed from a line that is not the fit.

Answer. $\;$(a) The error is omitting the centering corrections: $S_{xy}=\sum x_iy_i-n\bar x\bar y=19.7$, $S_{xx}=\sum x_i^2-n\bar x^2=10$, so $\hat\theta_1=1.97$, not $1.9945455$. (b) Yes — it is the least-squares slope of the no-intercept model, whose $\mathrm{SSE}=0.0983636>0.0910$. (c) The reported line minimizes no criterion at all and violates both residual identities.

Trap. Concluding "the two slopes are close, so it does not matter." The gap here is small only because $\hat\theta_0=0.09$ is nearly zero. Shift every $y_i$ up by $50$: the correct slope stays $1.97$ (a vertical shift changes only the intercept), while $\sum x_iy_i/\sum x_i^2$ jumps to $(109.7+50\cdot15)/55=15.63$ — a factor of eight wrong.

Tool: G7 §3.2 (normal equations, closed forms, the $S_{xx},S_{xy}$ notation) · G7 Practice 3.3 (regression through the origin) · B&T §9.2

Q147. ★★ · What the fitted line does not claim · G7 §3.8

Q145's pump fit is $\hat y=0.09+1.97x$ with $R^2=0.9976607$ on five settings $x\in\{1,2,3,4,5\}$. An engineer draws four conclusions. Mark each true or false, with a reason; where false, state what the data do support.

(i) "$R^2=0.998$, so the relation between speed and flow is essentially deterministic and linear over the whole operating range."
(ii) "Raising the setting to $x=12$ will deliver about $\hat y=23.73$ L/min."
(iii) "Since $R^2$ is so high, the slope is certainly not zero — no confidence interval needed."
(iv) "The relation is invertible: to get $y=10$ L/min I need $x=1/\hat\theta_1\times10$-ish, and regressing $x$ on $y$ would give slope $1/1.97=0.5076$."

Solution

Setup. A least-squares line is a summary of the data in the observed range of $x$, under a model $Y_i=\theta_0+\theta_1x_i+W_i$ in which the $x_i$ are fixed, chosen constants and only the noise is random (G7 §3.1). Every one of the four claims oversteps that in a different direction.

(i) False. $R^2$ measures how much of the observed spread $S_{yy}=38.90$ the line removes: $1-0.091/38.90=0.9976607$. It says nothing about (a) linearity outside the five settings tried, (b) whether a curve would do even better, or (c) the absolute size of the residual noise, which here is $\hat\sigma=\sqrt{\mathrm{SSE}/(n-2)}=\sqrt{0.091/3}=\sqrt{0.0303333}=0.1741647$ L/min. $R^2$ is also inflated by a wide spread of $x$'s: the same noise with the settings packed into $[3,3.2]$ would give a much smaller $R^2$ with an identical physical relationship. What the data support: over $x\in[1,5]$, a line fits with residual scale about $0.17$ L/min.

(ii) False — extrapolation. The arithmetic $0.09+1.97(12)=23.73$ is right; the inference is not. No datum was collected above $x=5$, so nothing in the fit constrains the shape of the relation there, and pumps saturate. Confidence intervals do not rescue you: they quantify noise around an assumed model, not the possibility that the model is the wrong shape outside its data (G7 §3.8). What the data support: predictions for $x$ within roughly $[1,5]$.

(iii) False. $R^2$ is not a significance statement; with $n=5$ points, three residual degrees of freedom, one needs the interval itself. With $\sigma$ unknown, G7 §3.6 gives $\hat\theta_1\pm t_{n-2,0.975}\,\hat\sigma/\sqrt{S_{xx}}$. Here $t_{3,0.975}=3.1824463$ and the standard error is $\hat\sigma/\sqrt{S_{xx}}=0.1741647/\sqrt{10}=0.0550757$, so the $95\%$ interval is $$1.97\pm3.1824463\times0.0550757=1.97\pm0.1752755=[1.7947245,\ 2.1452755].$$ It happens to exclude $0$ comfortably, so the conclusion survives — but it survives because the interval was computed, not because $R^2$ was large. (A dataset with $n=3$ and $R^2=0.99$ can easily have an interval straddling zero.)

(iv) False. Least squares is asymmetric: it minimizes squared errors in $y$ only. Regressing $x$ on $y$ minimizes a different criterion and gives slope $$\hat\beta_1=\frac{S_{xy}}{S_{yy}}=\frac{19.7}{38.90}=0.5064267,$$ not $1/1.97=0.5076142$. In fact the two slopes multiply to $\hat\theta_1\hat\beta_1=1.97\times0.5064267=0.9976607=R^2\le1$, with equality only for a perfect fit. The two lines coincide only when the points are exactly collinear. What the data support: to predict $y$ from $x$, use $\hat y=0.09+1.97x$; to predict $x$ from $y$, refit the other way; and neither is a statement that changing $x$ causes $y$ to move (G7 §3.8: only an intervention establishes that).

Answer. $\;$All four are false. $\hat\sigma=0.1741647$; $\hat y(12)=23.73$ is arithmetic, not evidence; the $95\%$ slope interval is $[1.7947245,\ 2.1452755]$; regressing $x$ on $y$ gives slope $0.5064267$, and $\hat\theta_1\hat\beta_1=R^2=0.9976607$.

Trap. The tempting answer is that (ii) is true "because the model is linear." The model is linear by assumption, and the assumption was only ever tested where data exist. A fitted line is an interpolation device with an extrapolation-shaped formula.

Tool: G7 §3.3 ($R^2$), §3.6 (reporting a regression; the $t$-interval for the slope), §3.8 (extrapolation, causation, asymmetry of the fit) · B&T §9.2

Q148. ★★ · A discrete LRT · G7 §4.2, §4.5

A supplier's process is either in control ($H_0$: each unit is defective independently with probability $p_0=0.1$) or drifted ($H_1$: $p_1=0.3$). You inspect $n=10$ units and count defectives $X$. (a) Derive the likelihood ratio $L(k)$ and show the LRT rejects $H_0$ for large $k$. (b) Evaluate $L(4)$ and interpret it. (c) Find the rejection region of the form $\{X\ge k\}$ with the largest attainable level not exceeding $\alpha=0.05$, and report the resulting $\alpha$ and $\beta$. (d) Why can you not achieve $\alpha=0.05$ exactly?

Solution

Setup. $X\sim\text{Binomial}(10,p)$ under both hypotheses, differing only in $p$. Its PMF is $p_X(k;p)=\binom{10}{k}p^k(1-p)^{10-k}$.

Step 1 — the ratio. The binomial coefficients are identical under the two hypotheses and cancel: $$L(k)=\frac{p_X(k;H_1)}{p_X(k;H_0)} =\frac{\binom{10}{k}(0.3)^k(0.7)^{10-k}}{\binom{10}{k}(0.1)^k(0.9)^{10-k}} =\left(\frac{0.3}{0.1}\right)^{k}\left(\frac{0.7}{0.9}\right)^{10-k} =3^k(0.7777778)^{10-k}.$$ Monotonicity: consecutive values satisfy $$\frac{L(k+1)}{L(k)}=\frac{0.3}{0.1}\cdot\frac{0.9}{0.7}=3\times1.2857143=3.8571429>1,$$ so $L$ is strictly increasing in $k$. Therefore $\{L(x)>\xi\}$ is always an upper tail $\{X\ge k\}$: rejecting for large $\xi$ is the same as rejecting for many defectives. This is what makes the abstract LRT usable — you never have to sort likelihood ratios by hand.

Step 2 — $L(4)$. $L(4)=3^4(0.7777778)^6=81\times0.2213773=17.9315653$. (Direct ratio of the two binomial PMFs: $17.9315653$. $\checkmark$) The observed count $4$ is about $17.9$ times more likely under drift than under control. For scale, $L(0)=(0.7777778)^{10}=0.0810131$, $L(2)=1.2052767$ and $L(3)=4.6489243$: the ratio crosses $1$ between $k=1$ and $k=2$.

Step 3 — calibrating to $\alpha\le0.05$. Compute upper tails under $H_0$ ($p=0.1$): $$\mathbf{P}(X\ge2)=0.2639011,\quad \mathbf{P}(X\ge3)=0.0701908,\quad \mathbf{P}(X\ge4)=0.0127952,\quad \mathbf{P}(X\ge5)=0.0016349 .$$ The smallest threshold whose tail does not exceed $0.05$ is $k=4$, since $\mathbf{P}(X\ge3)=0.0701908>0.05$ while $\mathbf{P}(X\ge4)=0.0127952\le0.05$. So $R=\{X\ge4\}$ and $\alpha=0.0127952$.

The type II error is the probability of not rejecting when $H_1$ holds: $$\beta=\mathbf{P}(X\le3;\,p=0.3)=0.6496107,$$ so the power is $1-0.6496107=0.3503893$. (Simulation with $400{,}000$ draws each returns $\hat\alpha=0.012705$ and $\hat\beta=0.650373$. $\checkmark$) Any critical value $\xi\in(L(3),L(4)]=(4.6489243,\ 17.9315653]$ produces this same test.

Step 4 — why not exactly $0.05$. $X$ takes eleven values, so $\mathbf{P}(X\ge k;H_0)$ takes only eleven values as $k$ ranges over the integers; $0.05$ is not one of them. It sits inside the jump from $0.0701908$ (at $k=3$) to $0.0127952$ (at $k=4$), a gap equal to $\mathbf{P}(X=3;H_0)=0.0573956$. Following the §4.5 rule — never exceed the promised level — you take $k=4$ and accept a test that is markedly more conservative than advertised. The price is visible in $\beta$: the $k=3$ test would have had $\beta=0.3827828$, roughly $0.27$ better power, but at $\alpha=0.0702$.

Answer. $\;L(k)=3^k(7/9)^{10-k}$, increasing in $k$; $L(4)=17.9315653$; $R=\{X\ge4\}$ with $\alpha=0.0127952$ and $\beta=0.6496107$ (power $0.3503893$); exact $\alpha=0.05$ is unattainable because $X$ is discrete.

Trap. Choosing $k=3$ "because $0.0702$ is closer to $0.05$ than $0.0128$ is." Closeness is not the criterion — the level is a promise, an upper bound on the false-rejection rate, and $0.0702>0.05$ breaks it. A second trap: reporting $\beta=1-\alpha=0.987$. $\alpha$ and $\beta$ are computed under different distributions and do not sum to anything in particular.

Tool: G7 §4.2 (likelihood ratio test, calibrating $\xi$ to $\alpha$), §4.5 (the discrete case: take the largest attainable level $\le\alpha$) · B&T §9.3, Example 9.14

Q149. ★ · What "not rejected" means · G7 §4.1

A researcher tests $H_0:\mu=0$ against $H_1:\mu=0.5$ using $n=9$ i.i.d. normal observations with known $\sigma=1$, rejecting when the sample mean $M_9$ exceeds a threshold calibrated to $\alpha=0.05$. She obtains $M_9=0.41$, does not reject, and writes in her report: "we conclude that $\mu=0$." (a) Find the threshold. (b) Find $\beta$, the probability of failing to reject when $\mu=0.5$ is in fact true. (c) Criticize her sentence and rewrite it. (d) What $n$ would push $\beta$ down to $0.10$?

Solution

Setup. Under either hypothesis $M_9\sim N(\mu,\sigma^2/n)=N(\mu,1/9)$, so the standard error is $\sigma/\sqrt n=1/3=0.3333333$. The rejection region is one-sided (larger $\mu$ means larger $M_n$), $R=\{M_9>c\}$.

Step 1 — the threshold. Calibrate under $H_0$: $$\alpha=\mathbf{P}(M_9>c;\,\mu=0)=1-\Phi\!\left(\frac{c}{1/3}\right)=0.05 \;\Longrightarrow\;\frac{c}{1/3}=z_{0.95}=1.6448536,$$ $$c=\frac{1.6448536}{3}=0.5482845 .$$ Her observed $M_9=0.41<0.5482845$, so indeed she does not reject.

Step 2 — $\beta$. Under $H_1$, $M_9\sim N(0.5,1/9)$, so $$\beta=\mathbf{P}(M_9\le c;\,\mu=0.5) =\Phi\!\left(\frac{0.5482845-0.5}{1/3}\right) =\Phi(0.1448536)=0.5575868 .$$ Equivalently $\beta=\Phi\big(z_{0.95}-\mu_1\sqrt n/\sigma\big)=\Phi(1.6448536-1.5)$. (Simulation of $500{,}000$ sample means gives $\hat\beta=0.55766$ and $\hat\alpha=0.049778$. $\checkmark$) The power is only $0.4424132$.

Step 3 — the critique. "We conclude that $\mu=0$" is unsupportable. Even if $\mu$ really were $0.5$ — the specific alternative she cares about — this test would have failed to reject $55.8\%$ of the time. Not rejecting is therefore only weak evidence, and none of it is evidence for $H_0$: the test was designed to control the false-rejection rate at $0.05$ and was never designed to control the false-acceptance rate at all. The asymmetry is deliberate (G7 §4.1): $H_0$ is the default, and the data either dislodge it or fail to.

A correct sentence: "at the $5\%$ level, the data do not provide sufficient evidence to reject $\mu=0$; with $n=9$ this test has power only $0.44$ against $\mu=0.5$, so the result is also consistent with a real effect of that size."

Step 4 — the $n$ for $\beta=0.10$. Require $\Phi\big(z_{0.95}-\mu_1\sqrt n/\sigma\big)\le0.10$. Since $\Phi$ is increasing and $\Phi(-z_{0.90})=0.10$, this is $z_{0.95}-\mu_1\sqrt n/\sigma\le-z_{0.90}$, i.e. $$\sqrt n\ \ge\ \frac{(z_{0.95}+z_{0.90})\sigma}{\mu_1} =\frac{(1.6448536+1.2815516)\times1}{0.5}=5.8528104,$$ $$n\ \ge\ (5.8528104)^2=34.2554\;\Longrightarrow\; n=35 .$$ Check: $\Phi(1.6448536-0.5\sqrt{35})=\Phi(1.6448536-2.9580399)=\Phi(-1.3131863)=0.0945601\le0.10$. $\checkmark$ Note the cost: detecting a half-standard-deviation effect reliably needs roughly four times her sample.

Answer. $\;c=0.5482845$; $\beta=0.5575868$ (power $0.4424132$); "we conclude $\mu=0$" is wrong — the correct statement is "insufficient evidence to reject at the $5\%$ level"; $n=35$ gives $\beta\le0.10$.

Trap. "Not rejecting at $\alpha=0.05$ means $\mathbf{P}(H_0)=0.95$." There is no probability attached to $H_0$ in classical inference — $\mu$ is a constant, not a random variable. Attaching one requires a prior and turns the problem into the Bayesian MAP comparison of G6 §3. A second trap: reading $\alpha=0.05$ as if it bounded the error rate of both decisions; here the other error rate is $0.5576$, eleven times larger.

Tool: G7 §4.1 (the two error types; why $H_0$ is privileged), §4.3 (test on a normal mean), §4.5 (the $\alpha$–$\beta$ tradeoff) · B&T §9.3

Q150. ★★★ · LRT, threshold, and the $\alpha$–$\beta$ tradeoff · G7 §4.2, §4.5

Component lifetimes $X_1,\dots,X_n$ are i.i.d. exponential. The manufacturer claims a failure rate $\lambda_0=1$ per year ($H_0$); a rival claims the parts are twice as good, $\lambda_1=1/2$ ($H_1$). You test $n=10$ components and observe the total lifetime $S=\sum_iX_i$. (a) Derive $L(x)$ and show the LRT rejects $H_0$ for large $S$. (b) With $\alpha=0.05$, find the threshold $\xi$ on $S$ (use: the $0.95$ quantile of a Gamma$(10,\text{rate }1)$ is $15.7052164$) and the corresponding threshold on the likelihood ratio itself. (c) Compute $\beta$. (d) Tabulate $(\alpha,\beta)$ for $\alpha=0.10,\ 0.05,\ 0.01$ using thresholds $14.2059903$, $15.7052164$, $18.7831174$, and state the tradeoff. (e) What $n$ makes $\beta\le0.05$ while keeping $\alpha=0.05$? (Gamma tail values are supplied below.)

Solution

Setup. The exponential density with rate $\lambda$ is $f(x;\lambda)=\lambda e^{-\lambda x}$ for $x\ge0$. Under $H_j$ the sum $S=\sum_{i=1}^{10}X_i$ is Erlang/Gamma with $n=10$ stages and rate $\lambda_j$ (G4 §3), with mean $n/\lambda_j$: $\mathbb{E}[S;H_0]=10$ and $\mathbb{E}[S;H_1]=20$. Longer total life is evidence for the rival's claim.

Step 1 — the likelihood ratio. By independence, $$L(x)=\frac{f_X(x;H_1)}{f_X(x;H_0)} =\frac{\prod_i\lambda_1e^{-\lambda_1x_i}}{\prod_i\lambda_0e^{-\lambda_0x_i}} =\left(\frac{\lambda_1}{\lambda_0}\right)^{n} e^{-(\lambda_1-\lambda_0)\sum_ix_i} =\left(\tfrac12\right)^{10}e^{\,S/2},$$ using $\lambda_1-\lambda_0=0.5-1=-0.5$. The data enter only through $S$ — $S$ is a sufficient statistic — and $e^{S/2}$ is strictly increasing in $S$. Hence $$\{L(x)>\xi_L\}=\{S>\xi\}\quad\text{for the matching }\xi,$$ so the LRT is "reject when total lifetime is large," as intuition demands.

Step 2 — calibrate $\xi$. Choose $\xi$ so the false-rejection probability is $\alpha$: $$\mathbf{P}(S>\xi;\,H_0)=0.05\;\Longleftrightarrow\;\xi=\text{Gamma}(10,1)\text{'s }0.95 \text{ quantile}=15.7052164 .$$ Check: $\mathbf{P}(S>15.7052164;H_0)=0.0500000$. $\checkmark$ On the sample mean this is $\xi/n=1.5705216$ years — reject if the average part lasts more than $1.57$ years, against a claimed mean of $1$. Translating to the ratio scale, $$\xi_L=\left(\tfrac12\right)^{10}e^{\,15.7052164/2} =0.0009765625\times e^{7.8526082}=0.0009765625\times2572.4350=2.5121436 .$$ So the test rejects when the data are more than about $2.51$ times as likely under $H_1$. That the threshold on $L$ exceeds $1$ is the classical asymmetry at work: a bare majority of evidence is not enough to dislodge $H_0$.

Step 3 — $\beta$. Under $H_1$, $S\sim$ Gamma$(10,\text{rate }0.5)$, mean $20$: $$\beta=\mathbf{P}(S\le15.7052164;\,H_1)=0.2652645 ,$$ so the power is $0.7347355$. (Simulation with $300{,}000$ replicates of each hypothesis returns $\hat\alpha=0.050167$ and $\hat\beta=0.265607$. $\checkmark$) Intuitively, $H_1$'s distribution is centered at $20$ and the threshold sits at $15.7052164$, only $(20-15.7052164)/6.3245553=0.6790649$ standard deviations below it (the Gamma standard deviation under $H_1$ is $\sqrt{10}/0.5=6.3245553$), so a substantial left tail falls below the threshold.

Step 4 — the tradeoff. Evaluating $\mathbf{P}(S\le\xi;H_1)$ at the three thresholds:

$$\begin{array}{c|c|c|c} \alpha & \xi & \beta & \text{power }1-\beta\\\hline 0.10 & 14.2059903 & 0.1801000 & 0.8199000\\ 0.05 & 15.7052164 & 0.2652645 & 0.7347355\\ 0.01 & 18.7831174 & 0.4640388 & 0.5359612 \end{array}$$

At fixed $n$, moving $\xi$ right shrinks the $H_0$ tail and grows the $H_1$ one: every factor-of-five reduction in $\alpha$ here roughly doubles $\beta$. There is no threshold that makes both small — the two error probabilities are linked by a single dial, and the only way off the curve is more data (G7 §4.5, §4.6).

Step 5 — the $n$ that buys $\beta\le0.05$. Increase $n$, recalibrating $\xi_n$ each time as the $0.95$ quantile of Gamma$(n,1)$ and evaluating $\beta_n=\mathbf{P}(S\le\xi_n;\text{rate }0.5)$. Scanning upward from $n=10$, the requirement first holds at $$n=23:\qquad \xi_{23}=31.4148102,\qquad \beta_{23}=0.0496647\le0.05 .$$ So $23$ components buy symmetric $5\%$ errors, against $10$ components with $\beta=0.265$. Note the shape of the answer: the separation between the hypotheses is fixed (a factor of two in rate), and both error probabilities shrink only as the sampling distributions pull apart at rate $\sqrt n$.

Answer. $\;L(x)=(1/2)^{10}e^{S/2}$, increasing in $S$; $\xi=15.7052164$ (equivalently $\xi_L=2.5121436$, or $1.5705216$ on the sample mean); $\beta=0.2652645$; the tradeoff is $(\alpha,\beta)=(0.10,0.1801),\ (0.05,0.2653),\ (0.01,0.4640)$; $n=23$ gives $\beta=0.0496647$ at $\alpha=0.05$.

Trap. Rejecting for small $S$ because "$H_1$ has the smaller parameter $\lambda_1=1/2$." The parameter is a rate, not a mean: a smaller rate means longer lifetimes. Getting the direction wrong flips the test into one with $\alpha=0.05$ and $\beta=\mathbf{P}(S>\text{lower quantile};H_1)$ close to $1$ — worse than tossing a coin. Always check the direction on the means ($10$ versus $20$) before calibrating anything.

Tool: G7 §4.2 (LRT, monotone ratio, calibrating $\xi$ to $\alpha$), §4.5 (the $\alpha$–$\beta$ tradeoff), §4.6 (Neyman–Pearson) · G4 §3 (Erlang as a sum of exponentials) · B&T §9.3

§8   Exam classics: multinomial, random walks, and waiting times

Drills G1 §4, G2 §3–§4, G4 §2, G5 §4 · Bertsekas–Tsitsiklis §1.4, §2.4–§2.7, §6.1, §7.4 · every number recomputed in computes/qb_s8.py

Twenty questions on the three families that reappear on every exam and that no single note section owns outright. Q151–Q156 are the multinomial block: a joint count vector, a single marginal count, what happens when you lump categories together, what happens when you condition on one count, why two counts are always negatively correlated, and a recognition item that separates multinomial from binomial from hypergeometric. Q157–Q163 walk the line: ruin probabilities fair and biased, expected duration, first passage on an unbounded walk, a walk with reflecting barriers, an item on what "fair game" does and does not promise, and a spot-the-error where a student solves $\pi P=\pi$ for a chain that has absorbing states. Q164–Q170 are waiting times: the first head, the $k$th head, then patterns — and the fact that $HH$ and $HT$ have different expected waits is the single most reliable surprise in this material. Q169 and Q170 close with the coupon collector, fair and unfair.

Q151. ★ · Multinomial joint probability · G1 §4.6

A supermarket has three checkout lanes $A$, $B$, $C$. Each arriving customer picks lane $A$ with probability $0.5$, lane $B$ with probability $0.3$, and lane $C$ with probability $0.2$, independently of every other customer. Twelve customers arrive. Let $(N_A,N_B,N_C)$ be the numbers who pick each lane. Compute (a) $\mathbf{P}(N_A=6,\,N_B=4,\,N_C=2)$, (b) $\mathbf{P}(N_A=N_B=N_C=4)$, and (c) $\mathbf{P}(\text{all twelve pick }A)$.

Solution

Setup. The twelve choices are independent and each has the same three-outcome PMF, so $(N_A,N_B,N_C)$ is multinomial with $n=12$ and $(p_A,p_B,p_C)=(0.5,0.3,0.2)$. Its PMF is $$\mathbf{P}(N_A=a,N_B=b,N_C=c)=\frac{n!}{a!\,b!\,c!}\,p_A^{a}p_B^{b}p_C^{c},\qquad a+b+c=n .$$ The two factors have separate jobs: $p_A^{a}p_B^{b}p_C^{c}$ is the probability of one particular sequence of choices with those counts (independence, so multiply), and the multinomial coefficient counts how many distinct sequences have those counts (G1 §4.4: partition 12 labeled customers into groups of sizes $a,b,c$). Every such sequence has the same probability, so the events are disjoint and equiprobable and we just multiply.

Step 1 (part a — the coefficient). With $(a,b,c)=(6,4,2)$, $$\frac{12!}{6!\,4!\,2!}=\frac{479001600}{720\cdot24\cdot2}=\frac{479001600}{34560}=13860 .$$

Step 2 (part a — one sequence). $$p_A^{6}p_B^{4}p_C^{2}=(0.5)^6(0.3)^4(0.2)^2=(0.015625)(0.0081)(0.04)=5.0625\times10^{-6} .$$

Step 3 (part a — multiply). $$\mathbf{P}(6,4,2)=13860\times5.0625\times10^{-6}=0.07016625 .$$ Monte Carlo over $400{,}000$ groups of twelve gives $0.069818\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part b). Now $(a,b,c)=(4,4,4)$, so $$\frac{12!}{4!\,4!\,4!}=\frac{479001600}{24^3}=\frac{479001600}{13824}=34650,$$ $$p_A^{4}p_B^{4}p_C^{4}=(0.0625)(0.0081)(0.0016)=8.1\times10^{-7},$$ $$\mathbf{P}(4,4,4)=34650\times8.1\times10^{-7}=0.0280665 .$$ Monte Carlo: $0.028623\;\checkmark$. Note this is smaller than the answer to (a): the equal split is not the most likely split, because the lanes are not equally likely.

Step 5 (part c). Here $(a,b,c)=(12,0,0)$, and the coefficient $12!/(12!\,0!\,0!)=1$ — there is exactly one such sequence. So $$\mathbf{P}(12,0,0)=(0.5)^{12}=0.000244140625 .$$

Answer. $\;$(a) $0.07016625$, (b) $0.0280665$, (c) $0.00024414$.

Trap. Writing $\mathbf{P}(6,4,2)=(0.5)^6(0.3)^4(0.2)^2=5.06\times10^{-6}$ and stopping. That is the probability of one specific ordering — customers 1–6 to $A$, then 7–10 to $B$, then 11–12 to $C$. The question asks for counts, which is $13860$ times more likely.

Tool: G1 §4.6 (multinomial coefficient, partitions into groups) · G2 §4.1 (joint PMFs) · B&T §1.6

Q152. ★ · Marginal of a multinomial · G2 §4.2

Same store as Q151: twelve customers, lane probabilities $(p_A,p_B,p_C)=(0.5,0.3,0.2)$. Ignore lanes $A$ and $C$ entirely and look only at $N_B$. (a) Name the distribution of $N_B$ and justify it in one sentence. (b) Compute $\mathbf{P}(N_B=4)$, $\mathbb{E}[N_B]$ and $\operatorname{var}(N_B)$. (c) Compute $\mathbf{P}(N_B\ge1)$.

Solution

Setup. To get a marginal from a multinomial you do not sum the joint PMF — you re-tell the story with fewer categories. Define, for customer $i$, the indicator $I_i=1$ if that customer picks $B$ and $I_i=0$ otherwise. Then $N_B=\sum_{i=1}^{12}I_i$.

Step 1 (part a). Each $I_i$ is Bernoulli with success probability $p_B=0.3$ (the customer either picks $B$ or does not — lanes $A$ and $C$ have been merged into the single outcome "not $B$"), and the twelve customers are independent. A sum of $n$ i.i.d. Bernoulli variables is binomial, so $$N_B\sim\text{Binomial}(n=12,\;p=0.3).$$

Step 2 (part b — the PMF value). $$\mathbf{P}(N_B=4)=\binom{12}{4}(0.3)^4(0.7)^8=495\,(0.0081)(0.05764801)=0.23113970 .$$ Monte Carlo over the same $400{,}000$ groups: $0.231405\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part b — moments). By linearity, $\mathbb{E}[N_B]=12(0.3)=3.6$. By independence the variances add, and each indicator has variance $p(1-p)$, so $$\operatorname{var}(N_B)=12(0.3)(0.7)=2.52,\qquad \sigma_{N_B}=\sqrt{2.52}=1.587451 .$$

Step 4 (part c). Complement: $N_B\ge1$ fails only when all twelve avoid $B$, and each avoids independently with probability $0.7$, so $$\mathbf{P}(N_B\ge1)=1-(0.7)^{12}=1-0.01384129=0.98615871 .$$

Answer. $\;N_B\sim\text{Bin}(12,0.3)$; $\mathbf{P}(N_B=4)=0.2311397$, $\mathbb{E}[N_B]=3.6$, $\operatorname{var}(N_B)=2.52$; $\mathbf{P}(N_B\ge1)=0.9861587$.

Trap. Using $\binom{12}{4}(0.3)^4(0.5)^{5}(0.2)^{3}$-style expressions, i.e. dragging the other lanes along. The whole point of a marginal is that the other categories are lumped into one; their internal split is irrelevant to $N_B$.

Tool: G2 §4.2 (marginals from a joint PMF) · G2 §1.4 (binomial PMF) · B&T §2.7

Q153. ★ · Lumping multinomial categories · G2 §4.2

Ten people independently answer a one-question survey with four options, chosen with probabilities $p_1=0.40$ (strongly agree), $p_2=0.25$ (agree), $p_3=0.20$ (disagree), $p_4=0.15$ (strongly disagree). Let $(N_1,N_2,N_3,N_4)$ be the counts and let $D=N_3+N_4$ be the number of "disagree of either strength" answers. (a) Give the distribution of $D$ and compute $\mathbf{P}(D=3)$, $\mathbb{E}[D]$, $\operatorname{var}(D)$. (b) Compute $\mathbf{P}(N_1=4,\,N_2=3,\,D=3)$.

Solution

Setup. Lumping rule. If you merge some categories of a multinomial into one, the result is again multinomial on the coarser category set, with the merged probability equal to the sum of the merged $p$'s. Reason: each trial still lands in exactly one coarse category, independently of the others, and by the additivity axiom the probability of landing in the merged category is $p_3+p_4$.

Step 1 (part a — the coarse model). Merging options 3 and 4, $$p_D=p_3+p_4=0.20+0.15=0.35 .$$ With only two coarse categories ($D$ and "not $D$"), multinomial reduces to binomial: $D\sim\text{Bin}(10,\,0.35)$.

Step 2 (part a — the numbers). $$\mathbf{P}(D=3)=\binom{10}{3}(0.35)^3(0.65)^7=120\,(0.042875)(0.04902228)=0.25221962,$$ $$\mathbb{E}[D]=10(0.35)=3.5,\qquad \operatorname{var}(D)=10(0.35)(0.65)=2.275 .$$ Monte Carlo over $400{,}000$ surveys gives $0.252410\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part b — three coarse categories). Now keep options 1 and 2 separate and lump 3 with 4, giving a three-category multinomial with probabilities $(0.40,\,0.25,\,0.35)$ summing to $1.00\;\checkmark$. Then $$\mathbf{P}(N_1=4,N_2=3,D=3)=\frac{10!}{4!\,3!\,3!}(0.40)^4(0.25)^3(0.35)^3 .$$

Step 4 (evaluate). The coefficient is $$\frac{10!}{4!\,3!\,3!}=\frac{3628800}{24\cdot6\cdot6}=\frac{3628800}{864}=4200,$$ and the probability of one such sequence is $(0.0256)(0.015625)(0.042875)=1.715\times10^{-5}$, so $$\mathbf{P}(4,3,3)=4200\times1.715\times10^{-5}=0.07203 .$$ Monte Carlo: $0.072370\;\checkmark$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $D\sim\text{Bin}(10,0.35)$, $\mathbf{P}(D=3)=0.2522196$, $\mathbb{E}[D]=3.5$, $\operatorname{var}(D)=2.275$; (b) $0.07203$.

Trap. Computing part (b) as a sum over the split of $D$ into $(N_3,N_4)$ — four terms $(3,0),(2,1),(1,2),(0,3)$ — is correct but slow, and easy to botch. Lumping first turns it into one line. (If you do sum, you must get $0.07203$; that is a good self-check.)

Tool: G2 §4.2 (lumping categories / marginalizing a joint PMF) · G1 §4.6 (multinomial coefficient) · B&T §2.7

Q154. ★★ · Conditioning a multinomial on one count · G2 §4.3

Twelve customers, lane probabilities $(p_A,p_B,p_C)=(0.5,0.3,0.2)$ as in Q151. You are told that exactly five customers picked lane $A$. (a) Derive the conditional distribution of $(N_B,N_C)$ given $N_A=5$. (b) Compute $\mathbf{P}(N_B=4\mid N_A=5)$ and $\mathbb{E}[N_B\mid N_A=5]$. (c) Compare $\mathbb{E}[N_B\mid N_A=5]$ with $\mathbb{E}[N_B]$ and explain the direction of the change.

Solution

Setup. Conditioning on $N_A=5$ does two things: it fixes the number of remaining customers at $12-5=7$, and it tells us each of those seven chose something other than $A$. Everything else about them is unaffected, because the twelve choices were independent.

Step 1 (part a — the conditional probabilities). For a customer known not to have picked $A$, the conditional probability of $B$ is, by the definition of conditional probability, $$\mathbf{P}(B\mid \text{not }A)=\frac{\mathbf{P}(B)}{\mathbf{P}(\text{not }A)}=\frac{0.3}{1-0.5}=\frac{0.3}{0.5}=0.6,$$ and likewise $\mathbf{P}(C\mid\text{not }A)=0.2/0.5=0.4$. These sum to $1.0\;\checkmark$. The seven remaining customers are independent, so $$(N_B,N_C)\mid\{N_A=5\}\;\sim\;\text{Multinomial}\bigl(7;\,0.6,\,0.4\bigr),$$ which with two categories is just $N_B\mid\{N_A=5\}\sim\text{Bin}(7,0.6)$.

Step 2 (verify the derivation algebraically). Divide the joint by the marginal. The joint at $(5,4,3)$ is $$\frac{12!}{5!\,4!\,3!}(0.5)^5(0.3)^4(0.2)^3=27720\,(0.03125)(0.0081)(0.008)=0.056133,$$ and the marginal $N_A\sim\text{Bin}(12,0.5)$ gives $$\mathbf{P}(N_A=5)=\binom{12}{5}(0.5)^{12}=792(0.000244140625)=0.19335938 .$$ The ratio is $0.056133/0.19335938=0.290304$, which we now confirm from the reduced model.

Step 3 (part b). From Step 1, $$\mathbf{P}(N_B=4\mid N_A=5)=\binom{7}{4}(0.6)^4(0.4)^3=35\,(0.1296)(0.064)=0.290304\;\checkmark$$ — identical to Step 2, as it must be. And $$\mathbb{E}[N_B\mid N_A=5]=7(0.6)=4.2,\qquad \operatorname{var}(N_B\mid N_A=5)=7(0.6)(0.4)=1.68 .$$ Monte Carlo, restricted to the simulated groups with $N_A=5$: $\mathbf{P}=0.289925$ and mean $4.20534\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). Unconditionally $\mathbb{E}[N_B]=12(0.3)=3.6$, so learning $N_A=5$ raises the expectation to $4.2$. The reason: $5$ is below the unconditional mean $\mathbb{E}[N_A]=12(0.5)=6$, so lane $A$ took fewer customers than usual and the surplus has to go somewhere; $B$ absorbs the fraction $0.6$ of it. Had we conditioned on $N_A=8$, the same formula would give $4(0.6)=2.4<3.6$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $(N_B,N_C)\mid\{N_A=5\}$ is multinomial$(7;0.6,0.4)$, i.e. $N_B\mid\{N_A=5\}\sim\text{Bin}(7,0.6)$; (b) $0.290304$ and $4.2$; (c) it rises from $3.6$ to $4.2$ because $N_A=5$ is below its mean $6$ and $B$ takes $60\%$ of the freed customers.

Trap. Keeping the original $p_B=0.3$ and computing $\binom{7}{4}(0.3)^4(0.7)^3=35(0.0081)(0.343)=0.097241$. Forgetting to renormalize by $\mathbf{P}(\text{not }A)=0.5$ makes the conditional probabilities sum to $0.3+0.2=0.5$, not $1$ — a fatal tell.

Tool: G2 §4.3 (conditional PMFs from joint PMFs) · G1 §2.1 (conditional probability as renormalization) · B&T §2.7

Q155. ★★ · Covariance of two multinomial counts · G3 §4.8

Twelve customers, lane probabilities $(0.5,0.3,0.2)$ as in Q151. (a) Compute $\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_B)$ and the correlation coefficient $\rho(N_A,N_B)$. (b) Explain, without algebra, why the sign must be what it is. (c) Verify your covariance using $\operatorname{var}(N_A+N_B)$ computed two different ways.

Solution

Setup. Write the counts as sums of indicators over the twelve customers: $N_A=\sum_{i=1}^{12}I_i$ with $I_i=\mathbb{1}\{\text{customer }i\text{ picks }A\}$, and $N_B=\sum_{j=1}^{12}J_j$ with $J_j=\mathbb{1}\{\text{customer }j\text{ picks }B\}$. Covariance is bilinear, so $$\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_B)=\sum_{i=1}^{12}\sum_{j=1}^{12}\operatorname{cov}(I_i,J_j).$$

Step 1 (kill the cross terms). For $i\ne j$ the two indicators involve different, independent customers, so $\operatorname{cov}(I_i,J_j)=0$. Only the $12$ diagonal terms survive.

Step 2 (the diagonal term). For a single customer, $I_iJ_i\equiv0$: one customer cannot pick both $A$ and $B$. So $\mathbb{E}[I_iJ_i]=0$ and $$\operatorname{cov}(I_i,J_i)=\mathbb{E}[I_iJ_i]-\mathbb{E}[I_i]\mathbb{E}[J_i]=0-(0.5)(0.3)=-0.15 .$$

Step 3 (part a — the covariance). Summing the $12$ diagonal terms, $$\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_B)=12\times(-0.15)=-1.8,$$ which is the general formula $\operatorname{cov}(N_i,N_j)=-n\,p_ip_j$ for $i\ne j$.

Step 4 (part a — the correlation). The marginals are binomial (Q152), so $\operatorname{var}(N_A)=12(0.5)(0.5)=3.0$ and $\operatorname{var}(N_B)=12(0.3)(0.7)=2.52$. Hence $$\rho=\frac{-1.8}{\sqrt{3.0}\sqrt{2.52}}=\frac{-1.8}{(1.732051)(1.587451)}=\frac{-1.8}{2.749545}=-0.654654 .$$ Equivalently $\rho=-\sqrt{\dfrac{p_Ap_B}{(1-p_A)(1-p_B)}}=-\sqrt{\dfrac{0.15}{0.35}}=-0.654654$, and note that $n$ cancels: the correlation does not weaken as the store gets busier. Monte Carlo over $400{,}000$ groups gives $\operatorname{cov}=-1.7893$ and $\rho=-0.6532\;\checkmark$.

Step 5 (part b — the reason for the sign). The counts are tied by the constraint $N_A+N_B+N_C=12$. The total is fixed, so a customer given to $A$ is a customer taken from $B$ (or $C$): the categories compete for a fixed budget of trials. Competition for a fixed total forces negative covariance between any two counts. Indeed every pair is negative here: $\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_C)=-12(0.5)(0.2)=-1.2$.

Step 6 (part c — the cross-check). First route: expand, $$\operatorname{var}(N_A+N_B)=\operatorname{var}(N_A)+\operatorname{var}(N_B)+2\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_B)=3.0+2.52+2(-1.8)=5.52-3.6=1.92 .$$ Second route: $N_A+N_B=12-N_C$ counts "not $C$", which by the lumping rule of Q153 is $\text{Bin}(12,\,0.8)$, so $$\operatorname{var}(N_A+N_B)=12(0.8)(0.2)=1.92\;\checkmark .$$ The two agree, which pins the covariance at $-1.8$.

Answer. $\;\operatorname{cov}(N_A,N_B)=-1.8$, $\rho(N_A,N_B)=-0.654654$; the sign is negative because the counts must sum to the fixed total $n=12$.

Trap. Concluding "$N_A$ and $N_B$ are built from independent customers, so they are independent, so the covariance is $0$." The trials are independent; the counts are not, because each trial feeds exactly one count. Using $\operatorname{cov}=0$ would give $\operatorname{var}(N_A+N_B)=5.52$, nearly three times the true $1.92$.

Tool: G3 §4.8 (covariance, correlation, bilinearity) · G2 §4.8 (indicator decomposition of counts) · G2 §4.7 (variance of a sum) · B&T §4.2

Q156. ★ · Which distribution is this? · G1 §4.3

An urn holds $20$ balls: $8$ red, $6$ green, $6$ blue. Consider two experiments. (E1) Five balls are drawn with replacement. (E2) Five balls are drawn without replacement. For each experiment, (a) name the joint distribution of the counts $(R,G,B)$ and the marginal distribution of $R$; (b) compute $\mathbf{P}(R=2,G=2,B=1)$; (c) state which experiment gives the larger answer in (b) and say why.

Solution

Setup. The single question that decides everything: are the draws independent and identically distributed? With replacement, yes — the urn is restored, so every draw is red with probability $8/20=0.4$, green with $6/20=0.3$, blue with $0.3$, independently. Without replacement, no — the composition shifts as balls leave, so the draws are dependent (though still exchangeable).

Step 1 (E1 — naming). With replacement, the counts are multinomial: $(R,G,B)\sim\text{Multinomial}(5;\,0.4,0.3,0.3)$. Lumping green and blue (Q153), the marginal is $R\sim\text{Bin}(5,0.4)$.

Step 2 (E1 — the number). $$\mathbf{P}(2,2,1)=\frac{5!}{2!\,2!\,1!}(0.4)^2(0.3)^2(0.3)^1=30\,(0.16)(0.09)(0.3)=30(0.00432)=0.1296 .$$

Step 3 (E2 — naming). Without replacement the counts follow the multivariate hypergeometric law: choose which balls, not which sequence. Its PMF is a ratio of binomial coefficients, $$\mathbf{P}(R=r,G=g,B=b)=\frac{\binom{8}{r}\binom{6}{g}\binom{6}{b}}{\binom{20}{5}},\qquad r+g+b=5 .$$ The marginal of $R$ is (univariate) hypergeometric: $8$ "successes" among $20$, sample of $5$.

Step 4 (E2 — the number). $$\mathbf{P}(2,2,1)=\frac{\binom{8}{2}\binom{6}{2}\binom{6}{1}}{\binom{20}{5}}=\frac{(28)(15)(6)}{15504}=\frac{2520}{15504}=0.16253870 .$$ A direct simulation of $200{,}000$ without-replacement draws gives $0.161710\;\checkmark$. For the marginals, $\mathbf{P}(R=2)$ is $\binom{5}{2}(0.4)^2(0.6)^3=0.3456$ under E1 and $\binom{8}{2}\binom{12}{3}/\binom{20}{5}=6160/15504=0.39731682$ under E2.

Step 5 (part c). E2 is larger, by a factor $0.16253870/0.1296=1.254157$. Sampling without replacement is self-balancing: once two reds are gone, red is scarcer and the remaining draws are pushed toward green and blue, so "spread out" outcomes like $(2,2,1)$ gain probability while lopsided ones like $(5,0,0)$ lose it. Formally, the hypergeometric has the smaller variance — the finite-population correction factor $(N-n)/(N-1)=15/19$ multiplies the binomial variance.

Answer. $\;$E1: multinomial$(5;0.4,0.3,0.3)$ with $R\sim\text{Bin}(5,0.4)$, $\mathbf{P}(2,2,1)=0.1296$. E2: multivariate hypergeometric, $R$ hypergeometric, $\mathbf{P}(2,2,1)=0.1625387$. E2 is larger because sampling without replacement suppresses lopsided counts.

Trap. Using multinomial probabilities for E2 "because $20$ balls is a lot". A sample of $5$ out of $20$ is a quarter of the population — the approximation is off by $25\%$ here. The multinomial is a good approximation to the hypergeometric only when the sample is a small fraction of the population.

Tool: G1 §4.3–§4.4 (ordered vs unordered sampling, with and without replacement) · G2 §1.4 (binomial) · B&T §1.6, §2.7

Q157. ★ · Symmetric random walk with two barriers · G5 §4.3

A hiker on a narrow ridge occupies integer positions $0,1,\dots,8$. Each minute she steps one unit right or one unit left with probability $1/2$ each, independently, until she reaches either end. Position $0$ is her camp and position $8$ is the summit; both end the walk. She starts at position $3$. (a) What is the probability she reaches the summit? (b) How many minutes does she walk on average? (c) Repeat both for a start at position $6$, and say in one sentence why (b) does not move in the same direction as (a).

Solution

Setup. This is the symmetric gambler's-ruin chain on states $\{0,1,\dots,N\}$ with $N=8$, absorbing at both ends. Let $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }N\mid X_0=i)$ and $D_i=\mathbb{E}[\text{steps until absorption}\mid X_0=i]$. First-step analysis gives, for $0<i<N$, $$a_i=\tfrac12a_{i-1}+\tfrac12a_{i+1},\qquad a_0=0,\ a_N=1,$$ $$D_i=1+\tfrac12D_{i-1}+\tfrac12D_{i+1},\qquad D_0=D_N=0 .$$ The first says the $a_i$ are in arithmetic progression (each is the average of its neighbors); with the boundary values pinned at $0$ and $1$ the unique solution is the straight line $a_i=i/N$. The second has solution $D_i=i(N-i)$, checked below.

Step 1 (part a). $$a_3=\frac{3}{8}=0.375,$$ so she reaches the summit with probability $0.375$ and returns to camp with probability $1-0.375=0.625$. Monte Carlo over $300{,}000$ walks: $0.375313\;\checkmark$.

Step 2 (part b). $$D_3=3(8-3)=3\times5=15\ \text{minutes}.$$ Verify the recursion at $i=3$ using $D_2=2(6)=12$ and $D_4=4(4)=16$: $$1+\tfrac12(12)+\tfrac12(16)=1+6+8=15\;\checkmark .$$ Monte Carlo: $15.0068\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part c). $$a_6=\frac{6}{8}=0.75,\qquad D_6=6(8-6)=6\times2=12\ \text{minutes}.$$ Starting at $6$ doubles the summit probability ($0.375\to0.75$) but shortens the walk ($15\to12$ minutes). The reason: duration depends on distance to the nearest exit, not on which exit. Position $6$ is only $2$ steps from an absorbing state, while position $3$ is $3$ steps from the nearer one. The longest walk starts at the midpoint, $D_4=4(4)=16$.

Answer. $\;$(a) $a_3=0.375$; (b) $D_3=15$ minutes; (c) $a_6=0.75$ and $D_6=12$ minutes — duration is governed by distance to the nearest barrier, so it peaks at the midpoint ($D_4=16$).

Trap. Answering (b) with "$8$ minutes, since she needs $5$ net steps to the right and $3$ to the left". Net displacement is not step count: the walk has zero drift, so the expected net displacement per step is $0$ and the walk wanders for $O(N^2)$ steps, not $O(N)$.

Tool: G5 §4.1–§4.3 (first-step analysis, absorption probability, expected time to absorption) · B&T §7.4

Q158. ★★ · Biased ruin probability · G5 §4.3

A market maker's inventory is an integer in $\{0,1,\dots,6\}$ lots. Each trading hour the inventory rises by one lot with probability $p=0.6$ and falls by one with probability $0.4$, independently, until it hits $0$ (sold out) or $6$ (full, no more room). She starts with $2$ lots. (a) Compute the probability she fills up before selling out. (b) Compare with the fair case $p=0.5$ and with the adverse case $p=0.45$, and comment on how sharply the answer responds to $p$.

Solution

Setup. Biased gambler's ruin on $\{0,\dots,N\}$, $N=6$, up-probability $p$, down-probability $q=1-p$. With $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{hit }N\text{ before }0\mid X_0=i)$, first-step analysis gives $a_i=p\,a_{i+1}+q\,a_{i-1}$ with $a_0=0$, $a_N=1$. Writing $\rho=q/p$, the increments $a_{i+1}-a_i$ form a geometric sequence with ratio $\rho$, and summing them with the two boundary conditions yields $$a_i=\frac{1-\rho^{\,i}}{1-\rho^{\,N}},\qquad \rho=\frac{1-p}{p}\ne1 .$$ Read $\rho$ as the odds against you per step: $\rho<1$ means the drift is upward.

Step 1 (the ratio). With $p=0.6$, $$\rho=\frac{1-0.6}{0.6}=\frac{0.4}{0.6}=0.666667 .$$ Since $\rho<1$, the inventory drifts up, and we expect $a_2$ to beat the fair-game value.

Step 2 (the powers). $$\rho^{2}=(0.666667)^2=0.444444,\qquad \rho^{6}=(0.666667)^6=0.087791 .$$

Step 3 (part a). $$a_2=\frac{1-\rho^{2}}{1-\rho^{6}}=\frac{1-0.444444}{1-0.087791}=\frac{0.555556}{0.912209}=0.609023 .$$ So she fills up with probability $0.609023$ and sells out with probability $1-0.609023=0.390977$. Monte Carlo over $300{,}000$ sessions gives $0.608960\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part b — the comparisons). Fair case: $\rho=1$, the formula degenerates and the correct limit is the linear one from Q157, $$a_2=\frac{2}{6}=0.333333 .$$ Adverse case $p=0.45$: $\rho=0.55/0.45=1.222222$, $\rho^2=1.493827$, $\rho^6=3.333505$, so $$a_2=\frac{1-1.493827}{1-3.333505}=\frac{-0.493827}{-2.333505}=0.211625 .$$ (The two negative signs cancel; when $\rho>1$ the formula is still valid, it just has a negative numerator and denominator.)

Step 5 (interpretation). The three answers are $0.211625$, $0.333333$, $0.609023$ for $p=0.45,\,0.50,\,0.60$. A $10$-percentage-point swing in $p$ nearly triples the answer. The reason is the exponent: $a_i$ depends on $p$ through $\rho^{\,i}$ and $\rho^{\,N}$, so small per-step advantages compound geometrically over the barrier distance. This is why casino edges of a percent or two are decisive over many rounds.

Answer. $\;$(a) $a_2=0.609023$ (sells out first with probability $0.390977$); (b) $0.333333$ at $p=0.5$ and $0.211625$ at $p=0.45$ — the dependence is geometric in $\rho=(1-p)/p$, not linear.

Trap. Defining $\rho=p/q=1.5$ instead of $(1-p)/p$. That inversion turns the favorable walk into an unfavorable one and returns $a_2=(1-2.25)/(1-11.390625)=0.120301$ — a factor of five wrong, and in the wrong direction. Sanity-check the sign of the drift against the fair value $i/N$ before trusting the number.

Tool: G5 §4.3 (gambler's ruin with bias) · G5 §4.1 (first-step analysis) · B&T §7.4

Q159. ★★ · Expected duration of a biased walk · G5 §4.2

Same inventory walk as Q158: barriers at $0$ and $6$, up-probability $p=0.6$, start at $2$ lots. (a) Compute the expected number of hours until the walk stops. (b) Compare with the fair walk $p=0.5$ from the same start. (c) Confirm your answer by an independent argument based on expected net displacement.

Solution

Setup. Let $D_i$ be the expected number of steps to absorption from state $i$. First-step analysis gives $D_i=1+pD_{i+1}+qD_{i-1}$ for $0<i<N$ with $D_0=D_N=0$. For $p\ne\tfrac12$ the solution is $$D_i=\frac{i-N\,a_i}{1-2p},$$ where $a_i$ is the absorption-at-$N$ probability of Q158. (When $p>\tfrac12$ the denominator is negative and so is the numerator, so $D_i>0$ as it must be.)

Step 1 (the pieces). From Q158, $a_2=0.609023$. Then $$N\,a_2=6(0.609023)=3.654135,\qquad 1-2p=1-1.2=-0.2 .$$

Step 2 (part a). $$D_2=\frac{2-3.654135}{-0.2}=\frac{-1.654135}{-0.2}=8.270677\ \text{hours}.$$ Monte Carlo over $300{,}000$ sessions gives $8.27662\;\checkmark$.

Step 3 (part b). For $p=0.5$, Q157's formula gives $$D_2=2(6-2)=8\ \text{hours}.$$ So the biased walk lasts slightly longer ($8.27$ against $8$) even though its outcome is far less uncertain. Bias is not the same as speed: the drift pulls her toward the far barrier at $6$, which is $4$ steps away, instead of letting her fall out quickly at $0$, which is only $2$ steps away.

Step 4 (part c — the displacement cross-check). Let $T$ be the stopping time and $X_T\in\{0,6\}$ the final inventory. Each step changes inventory by $+1$ with probability $0.6$ and $-1$ with probability $0.4$, so the mean drift per step is $$\mathbb{E}[\text{step}]=(+1)(0.6)+(-1)(0.4)=0.2 .$$ Wald's identity (the expected total displacement is the mean drift times the expected number of steps) gives $\mathbb{E}[X_T]-X_0=0.2\,\mathbb{E}[T]$. The left side is $$\mathbb{E}[X_T]-2=\bigl(6(0.609023)+0(0.390977)\bigr)-2=3.654135-2=1.654135 .$$ Hence $$\mathbb{E}[T]=\frac{1.654135}{0.2}=8.270677\;\checkmark,$$ matching Step 2 exactly. This is the same algebra as the $D_i$ formula, rearranged — which is why the formula has the shape it does.

Answer. $\;$(a) $D_2=8.270677$ hours; (b) the fair walk takes $8$ hours — slightly less; (c) Wald: $\mathbb{E}[T]=(\mathbb{E}[X_T]-X_0)/(2p-1)=1.654135/0.2=8.270677$.

Trap. Reasoning "the drift is $+0.2$ per hour and she needs $4$ more lots, so $4/0.2=20$ hours". That ignores the barrier at $0$, which absorbs $39\%$ of the paths early and pulls the average down by more than half. The drift argument is only valid once you use the expected final position $3.654135$, not the hoped-for one.

Tool: G5 §4.2 (expected time to absorption) · G4 §2.2 (expected value of a random sum) · B&T §7.4

Q160. ★★ · First passage on an unbounded walk · G5 §4.4

A walker starts at $0$ on the integer line, with no barriers at all. Each step is $+1$ with probability $p$ and $-1$ with probability $1-p$, independently. Let $T_k$ be the first time the walk reaches level $+k$. (a) For $p=0.55$, compute $\mathbb{E}[T_1]$ and $\mathbb{E}[T_5]$. (b) Explain why the answer to (a) becomes infinite when $p=0.5$, even though the walk still reaches $+1$ with probability $1$. (c) For $p=0.45$, compute $\mathbf{P}(\text{the walk ever reaches }+1)$.

Solution

Setup. Let $\mu=\mathbb{E}[T_1]$, the expected time to climb one level. Because the walk is spatially homogeneous (the step distribution does not depend on position) and has no memory, climbing from level $j$ to level $j+1$ is a fresh copy of the same problem. So the climbs are i.i.d. and $$\mathbb{E}[T_k]=k\,\mu .$$

Step 1 (first-step analysis for $\mu$). Condition on the first step. With probability $p$ it lands on $+1$ and we are done in one step. With probability $q=1-p$ it lands on $-1$, and from there we must climb two levels, which takes $2\mu$ more on average: $$\mu=1+q\,(2\mu)\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mu(1-2q)=1\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \mu=\frac{1}{1-2q}=\frac{1}{2p-1}.$$

Step 2 (part a). With $p=0.55$, the drift per step is $2p-1=2(0.55)-1=0.10$, so $$\mathbb{E}[T_1]=\frac{1}{0.10}=10\ \text{steps},\qquad \mathbb{E}[T_5]=5(10)=50\ \text{steps}.$$ Monte Carlo over $200{,}000$ walks gives $9.9609$ and $49.9036\;\checkmark$. Interpretation: the walk covers ground at exactly the drift rate $0.10$ levels per step, so reaching level $k$ takes $k/0.10$ steps on average — linear in $k$, unlike the $O(N^2)$ durations of the symmetric two-barrier walk in Q157.

Step 3 (part b). At $p=0.5$ the drift is $2(0.5)-1=0$ and the formula $\mu=1/0$ diverges. This is not a formula artifact. The symmetric walk is recurrent: it returns to every level with probability $1$, so $\mathbf{P}(T_1<\infty)=1$. But it is null recurrent: the tail of $T_1$ decays only like $\mathbf{P}(T_1>n)\sim c/\sqrt n$, which is heavy enough that $\mathbb{E}[T_1]=\sum_n\mathbf{P}(T_1>n)$ diverges. So the walk certainly gets there, but the expected time to do so is infinite — "always eventually" and "on average soon" are different statements.

Step 4 (part c). Now $p=0.45<\tfrac12$, so the drift is negative and the walk may never reach $+1$. Let $s=\mathbf{P}(\text{ever reach }+1)$. By the same homogeneity, reaching $+1$ from $-1$ requires two independent climbs, so it has probability $s^2$, and first-step analysis gives $$s=p+q\,s^{2}=0.45+0.55\,s^{2}.$$ Solving $0.55s^2-s+0.45=0$: the discriminant is $1-4(0.55)(0.45)=1-0.99=0.01$, so $$s=\frac{1\pm0.1}{1.1}\in\{1.0,\;0.818182\}.$$ The root $s=1$ is rejected: it is the correct root only when $p\ge q$. For $p<q$ the smaller root is the probability, giving $$s=\frac{p}{q}=\frac{0.45}{0.55}=0.818182 .$$

Answer. $\;$(a) $\mathbb{E}[T_1]=10$ and $\mathbb{E}[T_5]=50$ steps; (b) at $p=0.5$ the drift is $0$, the walk is null recurrent — it reaches $+1$ with probability $1$ but $\mathbb{E}[T_1]=\infty$; (c) $p/q=0.818182$.

Trap. Answering (b) with "the probability of reaching $+1$ is less than $1$, so the expectation is infinite". The probability is exactly $1$ at $p=0.5$. The infinity comes from the tail of a proper distribution, not from a defect of mass at $\infty$ — a distinction worth being able to state cleanly.

Tool: G5 §4.4 (mean first passage times) · G5 §4.7 (random walks on the integers) · G5 §2.1 (recurrence and transience) · B&T §7.2, §7.4

Q161. ★★★ · Random walk with reflecting barriers · G5 §3.4

A machine's backlog is an integer in $\{0,1,2,3,4\}$. Each hour the backlog rises by one with probability $p=0.4$ and falls by one with probability $q=0.6$, except at the ends, where the barriers reflect rather than absorb: at backlog $0$ a would-be decrease leaves the backlog at $0$, and at backlog $4$ a would-be increase leaves it at $4$. (a) Write the transition matrix. (b) Find the steady-state distribution. (c) What long-run fraction of hours is the machine idle (backlog $0$), and what is the long-run average backlog? (d) In one sentence, contrast this with the absorbing walk of Q158.

Solution

Setup. Reflection replaces absorption with a self-loop at each end. The chain is a birth–death chain: transitions move at most one step, so cutting the state space between $i$ and $i+1$ gives a local balance (detailed balance) equation. Every crossing upward must be matched by a crossing downward in the long run, because the chain can only get from one side of the cut to the other one step at a time: $$\pi_i\,p=\pi_{i+1}\,q\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad \pi_{i+1}=r\,\pi_i,\quad r=\frac{p}{q}.$$

Step 1 (part a). With $p=0.4$, $q=0.6$, states ordered $0,1,2,3,4$: $$P=\begin{pmatrix} 0.6&0.4&0&0&0\\ 0.6&0&0.4&0&0\\ 0&0.6&0&0.4&0\\ 0&0&0.6&0&0.4\\ 0&0&0&0.6&0.4 \end{pmatrix},$$ with row sums $1.0$ throughout $\checkmark$. The chain is irreducible (from any state you can reach any other) and aperiodic (the self-loops at $0$ and $4$ destroy periodicity), so a unique steady-state distribution exists and $\pi_j=\lim_n r_{ij}(n)$ for every $i$.

Step 2 (part b — the ratio). $$r=\frac{p}{q}=\frac{0.4}{0.6}=0.666667 .$$ Iterating local balance from $\pi_0$: $$\pi_i=r^{\,i}\pi_0,\qquad (r^0,\dots,r^4)=(1,\;0.666667,\;0.444444,\;0.296296,\;0.197531).$$

Step 3 (part b — normalize). Sum the geometric series: $$Z=\sum_{i=0}^{4}r^{\,i}=1+0.666667+0.444444+0.296296+0.197531=2.604938,$$ so $\pi_0=1/2.604938=0.383886$ and $$\pi=(0.383886,\;0.255924,\;0.170616,\;0.113744,\;0.075829),$$ which sums to $1.000000\;\checkmark$. Cross-check: multiplying an arbitrary starting vector by $P$ twenty thousand times reproduces this to $1.7\times10^{-16}$, and a $400{,}000$-hour simulated run gives time fractions $(0.38352,\,0.25503,\,0.17096,\,0.11437,\,0.07612)\;\checkmark$.

Step 4 (part c). The machine is idle a fraction $\pi_0=0.383886$ of hours, i.e. about $38.4\%$ of the time. The long-run average backlog is $$\sum_{i=0}^{4}i\,\pi_i=0(0.383886)+1(0.255924)+2(0.170616)+3(0.113744)+4(0.075829)$$ $$=0+0.255924+0.341232+0.341232+0.303318=1.241706\ \text{jobs}.$$

Step 5 (part d). In Q158 the ends were absorbing, so the interesting questions were "which end?" and "how long?" — the chain has no meaningful steady state, since it eventually sits forever at $0$ or $6$. Here the ends reflect, so the chain never stops, it is irreducible and aperiodic, and the interesting question becomes "what fraction of time in each state?". Same step probabilities, completely different toolset: absorption analysis versus balance equations.

Answer. $\;\pi=(0.383886,0.255924,0.170616,0.113744,0.075829)$; idle fraction $\pi_0=0.383886$; long-run average backlog $1.241706$.

Trap. Writing the boundary rows as $p_{00}=0$, $p_{01}=1$ ("at $0$ it must go up"). That is a different chain — a walk that is pushed off the barrier rather than one that stays when it tries to leave. It is periodic in the interior and gives a different $\pi$. Reflection as defined here keeps the downward attempt at $0$ as a self-loop, which is exactly what makes $\pi_0$ the largest entry.

Tool: G5 §3.2 (balance equations and normalization) · G5 §3.4 (birth–death chains, local balance) · B&T §7.3

Q162. ★★ · What "fair game" actually promises · G5 §4.3

Return to the hiker of Q157: symmetric steps, barriers at $0$ and $8$, start at $3$. (a) Without solving any difference equation, use the fact that the expected position never changes to derive $a_3=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }8)$. (b) A classmate says: "the game is fair, so she is equally likely to end at $0$ as at $8$." Say precisely what is wrong. (c) Now make the steps biased with $p=0.6$ up. Show what breaks in the argument of (a), and compute the correct $a_3$ and $D_3$; then verify them against the drift.

Solution

Setup. Let $X_n$ be the position and $T$ the absorption time. A single step has mean change $\mathbb{E}[X_{n+1}-X_n\mid X_n=i]=(+1)(p)+(-1)(1-p)=2p-1$ for interior $i$.

Step 1 (part a). With $p=\tfrac12$ the mean change per step is $2(0.5)-1=0$, so the expected position is constant in time: $\mathbb{E}[X_n]=X_0=3$ for every $n$, and the same holds at the (finite, with probability one) stopping time, $\mathbb{E}[X_T]=3$. But $X_T$ takes only the two values $0$ and $8$, so $$\mathbb{E}[X_T]=8\,a_3+0\,(1-a_3)=8a_3 .$$ Equating, $$8a_3=3\qquad\Longrightarrow\qquad a_3=\frac{3}{8}=0.375,$$ reproducing Q157 with no difference equation at all. This "conservation of expected fortune" is the fastest route to a symmetric ruin probability on an exam.

Step 2 (part b — the error). Fairness says the expected final position equals the starting position; it says nothing about the two outcomes being equally likely. Here $\mathbf{P}(X_T=8)=0.375$ and $\mathbf{P}(X_T=0)=0.625$ — she is ruined more often than not. Fairness is restored by the sizes of the outcomes, not their frequencies: she loses $3$ units with probability $0.625$ and gains $5$ with probability $0.375$, and $$(-3)(0.625)+(+5)(0.375)=-1.875+1.875=0\;\checkmark .$$ The classmate's claim would be right only from the midpoint $i=4$, where $a_4=4/8=0.5$.

Step 3 (part c — what breaks). With $p=0.6$ the mean change per step is $2(0.6)-1=0.2\ne0$, so $\mathbb{E}[X_n]$ is no longer constant — it grows by $0.2$ per surviving step, and the conservation argument gives no equation. Use instead the biased formulas of Q158 and Q159 with $\rho=(1-p)/p=0.666667$, $N=8$, $i=3$: $$\rho^3=0.296296,\qquad \rho^8=0.039018,$$ $$a_3=\frac{1-0.296296}{1-0.039018}=\frac{0.703704}{0.960982}=0.732276,$$ $$D_3=\frac{i-N a_3}{1-2p}=\frac{3-8(0.732276)}{1-1.2}=\frac{3-5.858208}{-0.2}=\frac{-2.858208}{-0.2}=14.291039 .$$

Step 4 (verify against the drift). The generalization of Step 1 is $\mathbb{E}[X_T]=X_0+(2p-1)\mathbb{E}[T]$ — the expected position moves at the drift rate for the expected number of steps. Substituting, $$X_0+(2p-1)\mathbb{E}[T]=3+(0.2)(14.291039)=3+2.858208=5.858208,$$ while directly $\mathbb{E}[X_T]=8(0.732276)+0(0.267724)=5.858208\;\checkmark$. The two agree, confirming both numbers at once.

Answer. $\;$(a) $\mathbb{E}[X_T]=8a_3=3\Rightarrow a_3=0.375$; (b) fairness fixes the expected final fortune, not the probabilities — the true split is $0.375/0.625$, made fair by the unequal stakes $+5$ and $-3$; (c) with $p=0.6$, $a_3=0.732276$ and $D_3=14.291039$, consistent with $\mathbb{E}[X_T]=3+0.2(14.291039)=5.858208$.

Trap. Trying to salvage (a) under bias by writing $8a_3=3$ anyway. That gives $0.375$ instead of $0.732276$ — you would predict she reaches the summit less than half the time in a walk that is pushing her uphill.

Tool: G5 §4.3 (absorption probabilities; the fair-game shortcut) · G5 §4.2 (expected absorption time) · B&T §7.4

Q163. ★★★ · Spot the error: steady state on an absorbing chain · G5 §2.4

A student studies the walk on $\{0,1,2,3\}$ with symmetric steps in the interior and absorption at both ends: $$P=\begin{pmatrix}1&0&0&0\\0.5&0&0.5&0\\0&0.5&0&0.5\\0&0&0&1\end{pmatrix}.$$ He writes: "I solved the balance equations $\pi=\pi P$ with $\sum_j\pi_j=1$ and found $\pi=(0.5,\,0,\,0,\,0.5)$. So the chain spends half its time at $0$ and half at $3$, and therefore, starting from state $1$, the probability of ending at $3$ is $0.5$." Identify every error, and give the correct probability of absorption at $3$ from state $1$ and the correct expected time to absorption.

Solution

Setup. The steady-state convergence theorem (G5 §2.4) requires a single recurrent class that is aperiodic. Only then is $\pi$ unique and equal to $\lim_n r_{ij}(n)$ for every starting state $i$. Check the hypothesis before using the conclusion.

Step 1 (error 1 — the class structure). This chain has two recurrent classes, $\{0\}$ and $\{3\}$, each a single absorbing state, plus a transient class $\{1,2\}$. With two recurrent classes the theorem does not apply, and $\pi$ is not unique. Indeed $(1,0,0,0)$ satisfies $\pi P=\pi$ (check: $(1,0,0,0)P=(1,0,0,0)\;\checkmark$), and so does $(0,0,0,1)$, and so does every mixture $(\alpha,0,0,1-\alpha)$ for $\alpha\in[0,1]$ — a whole line of solutions. The student's $(0.5,0,0,0.5)$ is just the $\alpha=0.5$ member; his solver picked it, the mathematics did not.

Step 2 (error 2 — the frequency claim). Even taking his vector at face value, "spends half its time at $0$ and half at $3$" is impossible for this chain: once it enters $0$ it stays at $0$ forever, so on any single realization the long-run fraction of time is $1$ at one end and $0$ at the other. His vector is not a time-average of any trajectory; it is at best an average across trajectories, which is a different object.

Step 3 (error 3 — the answer he wanted is a different question). Absorption probability depends on the starting state; a steady-state vector by construction forgets the starting state. That alone shows $\pi$ cannot answer it. The right tool is first-step analysis (G5 §4.1). Let $a_i=\mathbf{P}(\text{absorbed at }3\mid X_0=i)$, with $a_0=0$, $a_3=1$, and for interior states $$a_1=\tfrac12a_0+\tfrac12a_2=\tfrac12a_2,\qquad a_2=\tfrac12a_1+\tfrac12a_3=\tfrac12a_1+\tfrac12 .$$ Substituting the first into the second: $a_2=\tfrac12(\tfrac12a_2)+\tfrac12=\tfrac14a_2+\tfrac12$, so $\tfrac34a_2=\tfrac12$ and $$a_2=\frac{2}{3}=0.666667,\qquad a_1=\tfrac12(0.666667)=\frac{1}{3}=0.333333 .$$ This matches the symmetric formula $a_i=i/N=1/3$ from Q157. Raising $P$ to the power $4000$ numerically gives row $1$ equal to $(0.666667,\,0,\,0,\,0.333333)$ — the limit does exist here, it just depends on the starting row, which is precisely the failure of uniqueness.

Step 4 (expected time to absorption). With $\mu_i$ the expected steps from $i$, $\mu_0=\mu_3=0$ and $$\mu_1=1+\tfrac12\mu_0+\tfrac12\mu_2=1+\tfrac12\mu_2,\qquad \mu_2=1+\tfrac12\mu_1+\tfrac12\mu_3=1+\tfrac12\mu_1 .$$ Substituting: $\mu_1=1+\tfrac12(1+\tfrac12\mu_1)=1.5+\tfrac14\mu_1$, so $\tfrac34\mu_1=1.5$ and $\mu_1=2$; by symmetry $\mu_2=2$. Both match $i(N-i)$: $1(3-1)=2$ and $2(3-2)=2\;\checkmark$.
